The ride was long; it led up and down hills, and through a lengthy stretch of gloomy forest. Sometimes the drivers walked the horses up a steep climb and again raced them along a level bottom. Making a turn in the road they saw a bright light in the distance which marked their destination. In five minutes the horses dashed into a wide clearing. An immense log fire burned in front of a two-story structure. Streams of light poured from the small windows; the squeaking of fiddles, the shuffling of many feet, and gay laughter came through the open door.

===Chinese===

「真盼着她早成家早安顿。贝蒂在费城那会儿追求的人多，倒也没什么大碍；可在这边疆上，万万使不得。这些男人容不下这个。贝蒂再这样卖弄风情，是要出乱子的。」
「嗐，贝西，她还不过是个孩子。你要她怎么样？头一个开口的就嫁给他？」
「大爬犁来了。」雪橇铃铛的叮当声打破了寂静，赞恩太太说道。
上校一跃而起，打开房门。屋里一道宽阔的光柱射出去，照亮了路面。三挂壮实的马队停在门前，拉的是雪橇，本地人叫『大爬犁』——不过是架在木滑橇上的车斗罢了。上校一在门口露面，便迎来一片欢快的呼喊。
「好嘞！好嘞！她来了。」贝蒂跑下台阶时，他喊道。
上校把她安顿在头一挂雪橇的角落里，裹进野牛皮毯。她脚边搁了一只鹿皮袋，里头是一块烧热的石头——那是赞恩太太细心备下的。
「这边都齐了，开路吧。」上校喊道。「天气保准晴朗。回来的路上仔细检点挽索，留神狼群。」
长鞭甩得噼啪响，铃铛叮叮当当，急不可耐的马儿扬蹄发力，一行雪橇驶上晶莹的雪路。夜色清冷；黑沉沉的天穹上繁星眨眼；淡淡的月亮把冬日清辉洒向这银白冰封的世界。滑橇轻快平滑地一路飞掠，马蹄下扬起一阵阵细粉似的干雪，很快把雪橇里裹着黑皮毯的人也染白了。这一路下坡经过堡旁，过小溪桥，沿着黑森林边上的大道前行。路程很长；翻岗越谷，还要穿过一大段阴森森的林子。有时赶车人牵马步行上陡坡，有时又在平坦的谷底纵马飞驰。转过一个弯，远处一点亮光映入眼帘，那便是目的地。五分钟后，马儿冲进一片开阔的空地。一座两层木屋前燃着一大堆篝火。小窗里流泻出道道灯光；敞开的门里传出提琴的吱呀声、杂沓的脚步声和欢快的笑语。

===Original===

The steaming horses were unhitched, covered carefully with robes and led into sheltered places, while the merry party disappeared into the house.
The occasion was the celebration of the birthday of old Dan Watkins’s daughter. Dan was one of the oldest settlers along the river; in fact, he had located his farm several years after Col. Zane had founded the settlement. He was noted for his openhanded dealing and kindness of heart. He had loaned many a head of cattle which had never been returned, and many a sack of flour had left his mill unpaid for in grain. He was a good shot, he would lay a tree on the ground as quickly as any man who ever swung an axe, and he could drink more whiskey than any man in the valley.
Dan stood at the door with a smile of welcome upon his rugged features and a handshake and a pleasant word for everyone. His daughter Susan greeted the men with a little curtsy and kissed the girls upon the cheek. Susan was not pretty, though she was strong and healthy; her laughing blue eyes assured a sunny disposition, and she numbered her suitors by the score.
The young people lost no time. Soon the floor was covered with their whirling forms.
In one corner of the room sat a little dried-up old woman with white hair and bright dark eyes. This was Grandma Watkins. She was very old, so old that no one knew her age, but she was still vigorous enough to do her day’s work with more pleasure than many a younger woman. Just now she was talking to Wetzel, who leaned upon his inseparable rifle and listened to her chatter. The hunter liked the old lady and would often stop at her cabin while on his way to the settlement and leave at her door a fat turkey or a haunch of venison.
“Lew Wetzel, I am ashamed of you.” Grandmother Watkins was saying. “Put that gun in the corner and get out there and dance. Enjoy yourself. You are only a boy yet.”
“I’d better look on, mother,” answered the hunter.
“Pshaw! You can hop and skip around like any of them and laugh too if you want. I hope that pretty sister of Eb Zane has caught your fancy.”
“She is not for the like of me,” he said gently. “I haven’t the gifts.”
“Don’t talk about gifts. Not to an old woman who has lived three times and more your age,” she said impatiently. “It is not gifts a woman wants out here in the West. If she does ’twill do her no good.

浑身冒着热气的马匹被卸下套来，仔细盖好毯子，牵到避风的地方；欢欢喜喜的一行人则进了屋。
这场聚会是为老丹·沃特金斯（Dan Watkins）的女儿过生日。丹是沿河一带最老的移民之一；确切说，赞恩上校创建移民点几年之后，他才安下自己的农庄。他以手面大方、心地仁厚出名。他借出去的牛，许多头再没回来；他磨坊里出去的面粉，许多袋也没见拿粮食来抵账。他枪法好，抡斧放树不输给任何好手；论喝威士忌，全山谷没人喝得过他。
丹站在门口，饱经风霜的脸上堆着欢迎的笑，跟每个人握手、说句亲热话。他女儿苏珊（Susan）向男人们微微屈膝行礼，又亲亲姑娘们的脸颊。苏珊算不上漂亮，却结实健壮；一双爱笑的蓝眼睛透出开朗的性子，追求她的小伙子一数就是一打又一打。
年轻人片刻也不耽搁。不多时，旋转的身影已挤满了整个舞场。
屋子一角坐着一位干瘦矮小的老太太，满头白发，一双黑眼睛却亮晶晶的。这就是沃特金斯婆婆。她年纪很大了，大得没人说得清她的岁数，可身子骨还硬朗，做起一天的活计来，比许多年轻媳妇还带劲。这会儿她正跟韦策尔说话，猎人倚着那杆从不离身的来复枪，听她唠嗑。猎人喜欢这位老太太，去移民点的路上常在她的小屋歇脚，在她门口留下一只肥火鸡或一条鹿腿。
「刘易斯·韦策尔，我真替你害臊。」沃特金斯婆婆正说着。「把枪搁到墙角去，到那边跳舞去。痛快玩一玩。你还不过是个小伙子呢。」
「我还是在旁边看着的好，大娘。」猎人答道。
「呸！你能跟他们随便哪个一样蹦跶撒欢，想笑只管笑。我盼着埃布·赞恩那个漂亮妹妹已经入了你的眼呢。」
「她不是给我这样的人预备的。」他轻声说。「我没那个本事。」
「别说本事不本事。对一个比你多活了两辈还不止的老太婆，别提这个。」她不耐烦地说。「在这西部，女人要的不是本事。就是要了，对她也没好处。

===Original===

She needs a strong arm to build cabins, a quick eye with a rifle, and a fearless heart. What border women want are houses and children. They must bring up men, men to drive the redskins back, men to till the soil, or else what is the good of our suffering here.”
“You are right,” said Wetzel thoughtfully. “But I’d hate to see a flower like Betty Zane in a rude hunter’s cabin.”
“I have known the Zanes for forty year’ and I never saw one yet that was afraid of work. And you might win her if you would give up running mad after Indians. I’ll allow no woman would put up with that. You have killed many Indians. You ought to be satisfied.”
“Fightin’ redskins is somethin’ I can’t help,” said the hunter, slowly shaking his head. “If I got married the fever would come on and I’d leave home. No, I’m no good for a woman. Fightin’ is all I’m good for.”
“Why not fight for her, then? Don’t let one of these boys walk off with her. Look at her. She likes fun and admiration. I believe you do care for her. Why not try to win her?”
“Who is that tall man with her?” continued the old lady as Wetzel did not answer. “There, they have gone into the other room. Who is he?”
“His name is Miller.”
“Lewis, I don’t like him. I have been watching him all evening. I’m a contrary old woman, I know, but I have seen a good many men in my time, and his face is not honest. He is in love with her. Does she care for him?”
“No, Betty doesn’t care for Miller. She’s just full of life and fun.”
“You may be mistaken. All the Zanes are fire and brimstone and this girl is a Zane clear through. Go and fetch her to me, Lewis. I’ll tell you if there’s a chance for you.”
“Dear mother, perhaps there’s a wife in Heaven for me. There’s none on earth,” said the hunter, a sad smile flitting over his calm face.
Ralfe Miller, whose actions had occasioned the remarks of the old lady, would have been conspicuous in any assembly of men. There was something in his dark face that compelled interest and yet left the observer in doubt. His square chin, deep-set eyes and firm mouth denoted a strong and indomitable will. He looked a man whom it would be dangerous to cross.
Little was known of Miller’s history. He hailed from Ft. Pitt, where he had a reputation as a good soldier, but a man of morose and quarrelsome disposition.

===Chinese===

「她要的是一双能盖木屋的臂膀，一双端枪瞄得准的眼睛，一颗不知道怕的心。边地的女人要的是房子和孩子。她们得拉扯出男子汉来——把红种人赶回去的男子汉，垦地种田的男子汉——不然咱们在这儿吃苦受罪图个什么。」
「你说得对。」韦策尔若有所思地说。「可要眼看贝蒂·赞恩这样一朵花儿插进猎人粗陋的木屋，我又不忍。」
「赞恩家我认了四十年，还没见一个怕干活的。你要是肯收了那股追印第安人追疯了的劲儿，兴许真能娶上她。我承认，换了哪个女人也受不了那个。你杀的印第安人够多了，该知足了。」
「跟红种人打，是我改不了的。」猎人缓缓摇头说。「成了家，那股热病一上来，我又会撂下家跑出去。不，我这种人配不上女人。除了打仗，我什么都不行。」
「那怎么不为她打这一仗呢？别让这帮小子里的哪个把她拐走。你瞧她。她爱热闹，爱听人夸。我看你心里是有她的。怎么不去争一争？」
「跟她说话的那个高个子是谁？」猎人没作声，老太太又往下说。「瞧，他们进那间屋去了。他是谁？」
「他叫米勒。」
「刘易斯，我不喜欢他。我瞧了他一晚上。我知道我这老太婆爱抬杠，可我这辈子见过的人多了，那张脸不老实。他看上她了。她心里有他吗？」
「没有，贝蒂心里没有米勒。她不过是一身活泼爱玩。」
「你也许看走了眼。赞恩家个个是烈火性子，这丫头从头到脚都是赞恩家的种。去把她给我领来，刘易斯。我告诉你你有没有指望。」
「好大娘，也许天上有我一个妻子。人间是没有的。」猎人说，平静的脸上掠过一丝凄然的笑。
那位引得老太太议论的拉尔夫·米勒，走在随便哪一群男人里都惹眼。他那张黝黑的脸上有点什么，逼得人注意，又叫人捉摸不透。方方的下巴、深陷的眼睛、紧抿的嘴，透着一股百折不回的意志。他看上去就是个招惹不得的人物。
米勒的来历没人知道多少。他来自皮特堡，在那儿名声不坏，是个好兵，只是性情阴郁，好跟人斗气。

===Original===

It was whispered that he drank, and that he had been friendly with the renegades McKee, Elliott, and Girty. He had passed the fall and winter at Ft. Henry, serving on garrison duty. Since he had made the acquaintance of Betty he had shown her all the attention possible.
On this night a close observer would have seen that Miller was laboring under some strong feeling. A half-subdued fire gleamed from his dark eyes. A peculiar nervous twitching of his nostrils betrayed a poorly suppressed excitement.
All evening he followed Betty like a shadow. Her kindness may have encouraged him. She danced often with him and showed a certain preference for his society. Alice and Lydia were puzzled by Betty’s manner. As they were intimate friends they believed they knew something of her likes and dislikes. Had not Betty told them she did not care for Mr. Miller? What was the meaning of the arch glances she bestowed upon him, if she did not care for him? To be sure, it was nothing wonderful for Betty to smile﻿—she was always prodigal of her smiles﻿—but she had never been known to encourage any man. The truth was that Betty had put her new resolution into effect; to be as merry and charming as any fancy-free maiden could possibly be, and the farthest removed from a young lady pining for an absent and indifferent sweetheart. To her sorrow Betty played her part too well.
Except to Wetzel, whose keen eyes little escaped, there was no significance in Miller’s hilarity one moment and sudden thoughtfulness the next. And if there had been, it would have excited no comment. Most of the young men had sampled some of old Dan’s best rye and their flushed faces and unusual spirits did not result altogether from the exercise of the dance.
After one of the reels Miller led Betty, with whom he had been dancing, into one of the side rooms. Round the dimly lighted room were benches upon which were seated some of the dancers. Betty was uneasy in mind and now wished that she had remained at home. They had exchanged several commonplace remarks when the music struck up and Betty rose quickly to her feet.
“See, the others have gone. Let us return,” she said.
“Wait,” said Miller hurriedly. “Do not go just yet. I wish to speak to you. I have asked you many times if you will marry me. Now I ask you again.”
“Mr.

这天晚上，留心的人看得出米勒正被一股强烈的情绪煎熬着。他那双黑眼睛里闪着压了一半的火光。鼻翼异样地翕动，泄露了他强自按捺的激动。
一晚上他像影子一样跟着贝蒂。她的和气也许纵容了他。她常跟他跳舞，对他的陪伴显出几分偏爱。爱丽丝和莉迪亚让贝蒂这副样子弄糊涂了。她们是闺中密友，自以为对她的好恶知道几分。贝蒂不是亲口说过，她不喜欢米勒先生吗？既然不喜欢，她朝他抛去的那种俏皮眼色又算什么？当然，贝蒂爱笑不是什么稀罕事——她的笑向来大方得很——可从没听说她抬举过哪个男人。实情是，贝蒂把她的新决心付诸实施了：要做一个无忧无虑的姑娘所能做的最快活、最动人的样子，离那个害相思、盼着薄情郎的小姐形象越远越好。只可惜，贝蒂这出戏演得太好了。
米勒一会儿兴高采烈，一会儿又忽然出神，这情形除了韦策尔——他那双锐眼几乎什么也逃不过——旁人都不觉得有什么要紧。就算有人觉得，也不会说三道四。小伙子们多半都尝过老丹家上好的黑麦威士忌，他们红扑扑的脸和不寻常的兴头，并不全是跳舞跳出来的。
一轮对舞跳罢，米勒把他方才的舞伴贝蒂领进一间侧屋。灯光幽暗的屋子四壁摆着长凳，坐着些歇脚的跳舞人。贝蒂心神不定，这会儿直后悔不该来。两人不咸不淡地说了几句，音乐又起，贝蒂霍地站起身。
「瞧，别人都走了。我们回去吧。」她说。
「等等。」米勒急急地说。「先别走。我有话对你说。我问过你多少回，肯不肯嫁给我。现在我再问你一次。」
「米

===Original===

Miller, I thanked you and begged you not to cause us both pain by again referring to that subject,” answered Betty with dignity. “If you will persist in bringing it up we cannot be friends any longer.”
“Wait, please wait. I have told you that I will not take ‘No’ for an answer. I love you with all my heart and soul and I cannot give you up.”
His voice was low and hoarse and thrilled with a strong man’s passion. Betty looked up into his face and tears of compassion filled her eyes. Her heart softened to this man, and her conscience gave her a little twinge of remorse. Could she not have averted all this? No doubt she had been much to blame, and this thought made her voice very low and sweet as she answered him.
“I like you as a friend, Mr. Miller, but we can never be more than friends. I am very sorry for you, and angry with myself that I did not try to help you instead of making it worse. Please do not speak of this again. Come, let us join the others.”
They were quite alone in the room. As Betty finished speaking and started for the door Miller intercepted her. She recoiled in alarm from his white face.
“No, you don’t go yet. I won’t give you up so easily. No woman can play fast and loose with me. Do you understand? What have you meant all this winter? You encouraged me. You know you did,” he cried passionately.
“I thought you were a gentleman. I have really taken the trouble to defend you against persons who evidently were not misled as to your real nature. I will not listen to you,” said Betty coldly. She turned away from him, all her softened feeling changed to scorn.
“You *shall* listen to me,” he whispered as he grasped her wrist and pulled her backward. All the man’s brutal passion had been aroused. The fierce border blood boiled within his heart. Unmasked he showed himself in his true colors﻿—a frontier desperado. His eyes gleamed dark and lurid beneath his bent brows and a short, desperate laugh passed his lips.
“I will make you love me, my proud beauty. I shall have you yet, one way or another.”
“Let me go. How dare you touch me!” cried Betty, the hot blood coloring her face. She struck him a stinging blow with her free hand and struggled with all her might to free herself; but she was powerless in his iron grasp. Closer he drew her.
“If it costs me my life I will kiss you for that blow,” he muttered hoarsely.
“Oh, you coward! you ruffian!

勒先生，我谢过你，也求过你，别再提那件事，免得我们两个都痛苦。」贝蒂端容答道。「你偏要旧话重提，那我们连朋友也做不成了。」
「等等，求你等等。我说过，我不接受『不』字。我全心全意爱着你，我不能放弃你。」
他的声音低沉沙哑，激荡着一个强壮汉子的热望。贝蒂抬眼望着他的脸，怜悯的泪水涌了上来。她心软了，良心又隐隐刺痛。这一切本可以避免的吧？无疑她自己也有很大的不是。想到这里，她答话的声音放得又轻又柔。
「我把你当朋友喜欢，米勒先生，可我们至多只能做朋友。我很替你难过，也怪我自己，非但没帮衬你，反倒把事情弄得更糟。请别再提这个了。来，我们到那边去。」
屋里就剩他们两个。贝蒂话音才落，转身朝门口走去，米勒一闪身拦住去路。看见他那张煞白的脸，她惊得直往后缩。
「不，你还不能走。我不会就这么撒手。没有哪个女人能对我若即若离。你明白吗？这一冬你是什么意思？是你鼓励我的。你自己清楚。」他激动地嚷道。
「我原当你是个上等人。我还真费过气力替你分辩，跟那些显然没看错你真面目的人分辩。我不听你说了。」贝蒂冷冷地说。她转过脸去，方才的软心肠化作了一腔鄙夷。
「你*必须*听我说。」他压低嗓子说，一把攥住她的手腕，把她往后拽。这男人粗暴的情欲全被撩了起来。边地人的凶血在他心头沸腾。撕下面具，他露出了本来面目——一个边疆上的亡命徒。低垂的眉下双眼幽幽放光，唇边滚出一声短促的、孤注一掷的笑。
「我要叫你爱上我，我的骄傲的美人儿。迟早你是我的，用这样那样的法子都一样。」
「放开我。你敢碰我！」贝蒂喊道，热血涌上脸。她抡起空着的那只手，狠狠抽了他一记耳光，拼尽全力想挣脱；可在他铁钳般的手里，她无能为力。他把她拽得更近了。
「这一巴掌，豁出命来我也要亲你一下。」他哑着嗓子咕哝。
「啊，你这懦夫！你这恶棍！放开我，不然我要喊了。」

Release me or I will scream.”
She had opened her lips to call for help when she saw a dark figure cross the threshold. She recognized the tall form of Wetzel. The hunter stood still in the doorway for a second and then with the swiftness of light he sprang forward. The single straightening of his arm sent Miller backward over a bench to the floor with a crashing sound. Miller rose with some difficulty and stood with one hand to his head.
“Lew, don’t draw your knife,” cried Betty as she saw Wetzel’s hand go inside his hunting shirt. She had thrown herself in front of him as Miller got to his feet. With both little hands she clung to the brawny arm of the hunter, but she could not stay it. Wetzel’s hand slipped to his belt.
“For God’s sake, Lew, do not kill him,” implored Betty, gazing horror-stricken at the glittering eyes of the hunter. “You have punished him enough. He only tried to kiss me. I was partly to blame. Put your knife away. Do not shed blood. For my sake, Lew, for my sake!”
When Betty found that she could not hold Wetzel’s arm she threw her arms round his neck and clung to him with all her young strength. No doubt her action averted a tragedy. If Miller had been inclined to draw a weapon then he might have had a good opportunity to use it. He had the reputation of being quick with his knife, and many of his past fights testified that he was not a coward. But he made no effort to attack Wetzel. It was certain that he measured with his eye the distance to the door. Wetzel was not like other men. Irrespective of his wonderful strength and agility there was something about the Indian hunter that terrified all men. Miller shrank before those eyes. He knew that never in all his life of adventure had he been as near death as at that moment. There was nothing between him and eternity but the delicate arms of this frail girl. At a slight wave of the hunter’s hand towards the door he turned and passed out.
“Oh, how dreadful!” cried Betty, dropping upon a bench with a sob of relief. “I am glad you came when you did even though you frightened me more than he did. Promise me that you will not do Miller any further harm. If you had fought it would all have been on my account; one or both of you might have been killed. Don’t look at me so. I do not care for him. I never did. Now that I know him I despise him. He lost his senses and tried to kiss me.

===Chinese===

她正张口要呼救，忽见一条黑影跨过门槛。她认出那高大的身形是韦策尔。猎人在门口静立了一瞬，随即快如闪电般扑上前去。他一条胳膊只那么一撑，米勒便向后翻过长凳，轰隆一声摔在地板上。米勒好容易才爬起来，一只手扶着脑袋。
「卢，别拔刀。」贝蒂看见韦策尔的手伸进猎衫，急得喊出声。米勒起身时，她已抢身挡在猎人跟前。两只小手死死拽住猎人粗壮的胳膊，可怎么也拽不住。韦策尔的手滑向了腰带。
「看在上帝份上，卢，别杀他。」贝蒂哀求道，惊恐地望着猎人那双寒光闪闪的眼睛。「你惩罚他够重的了。他不过是想亲我。我自己也有不是。把刀收起来。别见血。看在我的份上，卢，看在我的份上！」
贝蒂见拉不住韦策尔的胳膊，便搂住他的脖子，用她少女的全部力气吊住他。毫无疑问，她这一搂避免了一场惨祸。倘若米勒当时想拔家伙，那本是个下手的好机会。他是出了名的出手快，从前多次争斗也证明他并非懦夫。可他没有攻击韦策尔的意思。显然，他用眼睛估量着到门口的距离。韦策尔不是寻常人。且不说他惊人的膂力和身手，这个印第安人猎手身上有种叫所有人胆寒的东西。在那双眼睛的逼视下，米勒退缩了。他知道，在他一生的亡命生涯里，从没有一刻像此刻这样贴近死亡。隔在他与黄泉之间的，只有这个纤弱姑娘的一双细臂。猎人朝门口微微一挥手，他便转身出去了。
「啊，吓死人了！」贝蒂一屁股坐在长凳上，如释重负地抽泣了一声。「亏得你来得及时，尽管你比他吓人多了。答应我，别再跟米勒过不去。你们真要动起手来，全是为了我；你们两个，说不定哪个就没命了。别那样看我。我不喜欢他。从来就不喜欢。如今我看清了他，我鄙视他。他一时昏了头，想亲我。我恨不得亲手杀了他。」

===Original===

I could have killed him myself.”
Wetzel did not answer. Betty had been holding his hand in both her own while she spoke impulsively.
“I understand how difficult it is for you to overlook an insult to me,” she continued earnestly. “But I ask it of you. You are my best friend, almost my brother, and I promise you that if he ever speaks a word to me again that is not what it should be I will tell you.”
“I reckon I’ll let him go, considerin’ how set on it you are.”
“But remember, Lew, that he is revengeful and you must be on the lookout,” said Betty gravely as she recalled the malignant gleam in Miller’s eyes.
“He’s dangerous only like a moccasin snake that hides in the grass.”
“Am I all right? Do I look mussed or﻿—or excited﻿—or anything?” asked Betty.
Lewis smiled as she turned round for his benefit. Her hair was a little awry and the lace at her neck disarranged. The natural bloom had not quite returned to her cheeks. With a look in his eyes that would have mystified Betty for many a day had she but seen it he ran his gaze over the dainty figure. Then reassuring her that she looked as well as ever, he led her into the dance room.
“So this is Betty Zane. Dear child, kiss me,” said Grandmother Watkins when Wetzel had brought Betty up to her. “Now, let me get a good look at you. Well, well, you are a true Zane. Black hair and eyes; all fire and pride. Child, I knew your father and mother long before you were born. Your father was a fine man but a proud one. And how do you like the frontier? Are you enjoying yourself?”
“Oh, yes, indeed,” said Betty, smiling brightly at the old lady.
“Well, dearie, have a good time while you can. Life is hard in a pioneer’s cabin. You will not always have the Colonel to look after you. They tell me you have been to some grand school in Philadelphia. Learning is very well, but it will not help you in the cabin of one of these rough men.”
“There is a great need of education in all the pioneers’ homes. I have persuaded brother Eb to have a schoolteacher at the Fort next spring.”
“First teach the boys to plow and the girls to make Johnny cake. How much you favor your brother Isaac. He used to come and see me often. So must you in summertime. Poor lad, I suppose he is dead by this time. I have seen so many brave and good lads go. There now, I did not mean to make you sad,” and the old lady patted Betty’s hand and sighed.

===Chinese===

韦策尔没有作声。贝蒂方才说得激动，一直把他的手攥在自己两只手里。
「我知道，要你咽下我这口气受的辱，有多难。」她恳切地接着说。「可我求你了。你是我最好的朋友，跟亲哥哥差不多。我答应你，往后他要是再对我说一句不三不四的话，我一定告诉你。」
「既然你这么拦着，我看就放他一马吧。」
「可你要记住，卢，他记仇，你得时时防着他。」贝蒂想起米勒眼里恶毒的凶光，郑重地说。
「他那种危险，就像藏在草里的蝮蛇。」
「我这模样还行吗？头发乱不乱？——神色慌不慌？——没什么吧？」贝蒂问。
贝蒂转过身来让他打量，刘易斯微微一笑。她的头发有点散乱，领口的蕾丝也扯歪了。脸颊上天然的红晕还没完全回来。他的目光扫过她娇小的身影，眼里带着一种神情——贝蒂若是瞧见了，往后许多天都要琢磨不透。随后他安慰她说她好看得很，便领着她回到舞厅。
「这就是贝蒂·赞恩啊。好孩子，亲亲我。」韦策尔把贝蒂领到跟前时，沃特金斯婆婆说。「来，让我好好瞧瞧你。嗬，嗬，真是地道的赞恩家人。黑头发黑眼睛；一身的火性子和傲气。孩子，你出生前好多年，我就认识你爸爸妈妈了。你父亲是条好汉，就是傲。你觉得边疆怎么样？过得快活吗？」
「哦，快活，真的。」贝蒂朝老太太嫣然一笑。
「嗯，乖孩子，能乐且乐吧。拓荒者的木屋里，日子苦着呢。上校可不能护你一辈子。听说你在费城上过顶好的学堂。念书是好事，可到了这些粗汉子的木屋里，帮不上你什么。」
「拓荒者家家户户都缺教育。我已经说动哥哥埃布，明年春天在堡里请个教书先生。」
「先教男孩儿犁地，教女孩儿烙玉米饼吧。你跟你哥哥艾萨克长得真像。他从前常来看我。夏天你也得来。可怜的孩子，我估摸这会儿他已经不在人世了。眼看着多少勇敢的好小伙子走了。瞧我，本不该惹你伤心的。」老太太拍拍贝蒂的手，叹了口气。

===Original===

“He often spoke of you and said that I must come with him to see you. Now he is gone,” said Betty.
“Yes, he is gone, Betty, but you must not be sad while you are so young. Wait until you are old like I am. How long have you known Lew Wetzel?”
“All my life. He used to carry me in his arms when I was a baby. Of course I do not remember that, but as far back as I can go in memory I can see Lew. Oh, the many times he has saved me from disaster! But why do you ask?”
“I think Lew Wetzel cares more for you than for all the world. He is as silent as an Indian, but I am an old woman and I can read men’s hearts. If he could be made to give up his wandering life he would be the best man on the border.”
“Oh, indeed I think you are wrong. Lew does not care for me in that way,” said Betty, surprised and troubled by the old lady’s vehemence.
A loud blast from a hunting horn directed the attention of all to the platform at the upper end of the hall, where Dan Watkins stood. The fiddlers ceased playing, the dancers stopped, and all looked expectantly. The scene was simple, strong, and earnest. The light in the eyes of these maidens shone like the light from the pine cones on the walls. It beamed soft and warm. These fearless sons of the wilderness, these sturdy sons of progress, standing there clasping the hands of their partners and with faces glowing with happiness, forgetful of all save the enjoyment of the moment, were ready to go out on the morrow and battle unto the death for the homes and the lives of their loved ones.
“Friends,” said Dan when the hum of voices had ceased, “I never thought as how I’d have to get up here and make a speech tonight or I might have taken to the woods. Howsomever, mother and Susan says as it’s gettin’ late it’s about time we had some supper. Somewhere in the big cake is hid a gold ring. If one of the girls gets it she can keep it as a gift from Susan, and should one of the boys find it he may make a present to his best girl. And in the bargain he gets to kiss Susan. She made some objection about this and said that part of the game didn’t go, but I reckon the lucky young man will decide that for hisself. And now to the festal board.”
Ample justice was done to the turkey, the venison, and the bear meat. Grandmother Watkins’s delicious apple and pumpkin pies, for which she was renowned, disappeared as by magic.

===Chinese===

「他常说起你，还说一定要带我来看你。如今他不在了。」贝蒂说。
「是啊，他不在了，贝蒂。可你这么年轻，不该伤心。等你熬到我这把年纪再说吧。你认识卢·韦策尔多久了？」
「打我生下来就认识。我还是吃奶的娃娃时，他常抱着我。那当然不记得了，可记忆里再往前追，追到哪里都有卢。啊，他多少回救我脱险！可您问这个做什么？」
「我看卢·韦策尔把你看得比全世界都重。他闷得像印第安人，可我是老太婆了，男人的心思我读得懂。要是有人能劝他撇下那种四处漂泊的日子，他就是全边疆最好的男人。」
「啊，我看您弄错了。卢对我没有那个意思。」贝蒂说，老太太这番斩钉截铁的话叫她又惊讶又不安。
一声嘹亮的猎号，把众人的目光引向大厅顶头的平台，丹·沃特金斯站在那里。琴师住了手，跳舞的停下脚步，人人都期待地望着。眼前的场面朴素、有力而真挚。少女们眼里的光，就像墙上松果灯盏映出的光一样闪亮，柔和而温暖。这些荒野里无所畏惧的儿子，这些坚毅的拓荒之子，此刻握着舞伴的手，脸上焕发着幸福的光彩，除了眼前这份欢乐把什么都忘了，而明天一早，他们随时准备出去，为家园、为亲人的性命血战到底。
「各位街坊，」人声静下来后丹开口说，「我压根儿没料到今儿晚上还得爬上这儿讲话，早知道我就躲进林子去了。不过呢，老婆子和苏珊说，天不早了，该吃宵夜了。大蛋糕里藏着一枚金戒指。哪位姑娘吃着了，就留下吧，算苏珊送的礼；哪个小伙子找着了，可以拿它送给他最心爱的姑娘。另外搭上一份彩头——他可以亲苏珊一口。这一条苏珊有点儿意见，说这场戏里没这一出，可我寻思，走运的年轻人自会拿主意。好了，开席吧！」
火鸡、鹿肉、熊肉，个个吃得酒足饭饱。沃特金斯婆婆远近闻名的苹果派和南瓜派，像变戏法似的转眼一扫而光。各色糕点、甜苹果酒和苹果酱，也同样消失得无影无踪。

===Original===

Likewise the cakes and the sweet cider and the apple butter vanished.
When the big cake had been cut and divided among the guests, Wetzel discovered the gold ring within his share. He presented the ring to Betty, and gave his privilege of kissing Susan to George Reynolds, with the remark: “George, I calkilate Susan would like it better if you do the kissin’ part.” Now it was known to all that George had long been an ardent admirer of Susan’s, and it was suspected that she was not indifferent to him. Nevertheless, she protested that it was not fair. George acted like a man who had the opportunity of his life. Amid uproarious laughter he ran Susan all over the room, and when he caught her he pulled her hands away from her blushing face and bestowed a right hearty kiss on her cheek. To everyone’s surprise and to Wetzel’s discomfiture, Susan walked up to him and saying that as he had taken such an easy way out of it she intended to punish him by kissing him. And so she did. Poor Lewis’s face looked the picture of dismay. Probably he had never been kissed before in his life.
Happy hours sped away on the wings of the wind. The feasting over, the goodbyes were spoken, the girls were wrapped in the warm robes, for it was now intensely cold, and soon the horses, eager to start on the long homeward journey, were pulling hard on their bits. On the party’s return trip there was an absence of the hilarity which had prevailed on their coming. The bells were taken off before the sleds left the blockhouse, and the traces and the harness examined and tightened with the caution of men who were apprehensive of danger and who would take no chances.
In winter time the foes most feared by the settlers were the timber wolves. Thousands of these savage beasts infested the wild forest regions which bounded the lonely roads, and their wonderful power of scent and swift and tireless pursuit made a long night ride a thing to be dreaded. While the horses moved swiftly danger from wolves was not imminent; but carelessness or some mishap to a trace or a wheel had been the cause of more than one tragedy.
Therefore it was not remarkable that the drivers of our party breathed a sigh of relief when the top of the last steep hill had been reached. The girls were quiet, and tired out and cold they pressed close to one another; the men were silent and watchful.

===Chinese===

大蛋糕切开分给客人后，韦策尔在自己那份里发现了金戒指。他把戒指送给了贝蒂，又把亲苏珊的特权转让给乔治·雷诺兹，还撂下一句：「乔治，我寻思那亲嘴的一段，还是你来办，苏珊更乐意。」谁都知道，乔治早就火热地追求苏珊，大家也疑心苏珊对他并非无情。话虽如此，苏珊还是抗议这不公平。乔治一副得了平生头等好机会的做派。他在满堂哄笑中追着苏珊满屋子跑，逮住之后，掰开她捂着通红脸蛋的双手，在她脸颊上结结实实亲了一大口。出乎所有人意料、也叫韦策尔好生狼狈的是，苏珊走到他跟前，说他捡了这么个现成便宜，她要罚他——亲他一口。她说到做到。可怜的刘易斯，那张脸活脱脱一幅大惊失色的画。只怕他这辈子还没被人亲过。
幸福的时光乘风飞逝。宴席散了，道别的话说过了，姑娘们裹进暖和的皮毯——这时天已冷得厉害。不多时，急于踏上漫长归途的马儿绷紧了嚼子。一行人归途上，再没有来时那股热闹劲儿。雪橇还没离开木堡，铃铛就都摘了，挽索和马具也都查过、紧过，那份小心，活像一群预感到危险、不敢有半点大意的汉子。
冬天里，移民们最怕的仇敌是大灰狼。成千上万头这样的猛兽盘踞在夹着偏僻道路的莽莽林海里，它们灵敏无比的嗅觉，加上迅捷而不倦的追逐，叫夜里的长途赶路成了一桩可怕的事。只要马跑得飞快，狼群的威胁还不算迫在眉睫；可是一时大意，或是一条挽索、一个轮子出了岔子，酿成的惨剧已不止一桩。
因此，一行人爬上最后一道陡坡的坡顶时，赶车人不约而同松了口气，也就不足为怪了。姑娘们安安静静，又乏又冷，紧紧偎在一处；男人们一声不响，眼睛四下里提防着。

===Original===

When they were halfway home and had just reached the outskirts of the Black Forest the keen ear of Wetzel caught the cry of a wolf. It came from the south and sounded so faint that Wetzel believed at first that he had been mistaken. A few moments passed in which the hunter turned his ear to the south. He had about made up his mind that he had only imagined he had heard something when the unmistakable yelp of a wolf came down on the wind. Then another, this time clear and distinct, caused the driver to turn and whisper to Wetzel. The hunter spoke in a low tone and the driver whipped up his horses. From out the depths of the dark woods along which they were riding came a long and mournful howl. It was a wolf answering the call of his mate. This time the horses heard it, for they threw back their ears and increased their speed. The girls heard it, for they shrank closer to the men.
There is that which is frightful in the cry of a wolf. When one is safe in camp before a roaring fire the short, sharp bark of a wolf is startling, and the long howl will make one shudder. It is so lonely and dismal. It makes no difference whether it be given while the wolf is sitting on his haunches near some cabin waiting for the remains of the settler’s dinner, or while he is in full chase after his prey﻿—the cry is equally wild, savage and bloodcurdling.
Betty had never heard it and though she was brave, when the howl from the forest had its answer in another howl from the creek thicket, she slipped her little mittened hand under Wetzel’s arm and looked up at him with frightened eyes.
In half an hour the full chorus of yelps, barks and howls swelled hideously on the air, and the ever increasing pack of wolves could be seen scarcely a hundred yards behind the sleds. The patter of their swiftly flying feet on the snow could be distinctly heard. The slender, dark forms came nearer and nearer every moment. Presently the wolves had approached close enough for the occupants of the sleds to see their shining eyes looking like little balls of green fire. A gaunt beast bolder than the others, and evidently the leader of the pack, bounded forward until he was only a few yards from the last sled. At every jump he opened his great jaws and uttered a quick bark as if to embolden his followers.

===Chinese===

走到半路，刚到黑森林边上，韦策尔那双尖耳朵捕到了一声狼嗥。声音打南边来，微弱得很，起初韦策尔还当自己听岔了。猎人侧耳朝南听了一会儿。他刚打定主意认作幻觉，风里又送来一声错不了的狼嗥。接着又是一声，这回清清楚楚，赶车人回头朝韦策尔低低说了一句。猎人低声吩咐了句什么，赶车人扬鞭催马。他们正沿着赶路的那片黑魆魆的密林深处，传来一声悠长而凄厉的嗥叫。那是一头狼在应和同伴的呼唤。这回马也听见了——它们抿起耳朵，撒开了步子。姑娘们也听见了——她们往男人身边偎得更紧。
狼的叫声里有一种叫人胆寒的东西。就算人稳稳坐在营地熊熊的篝火前，那一声短促尖利的狼吠也叫人心头一惊，那拖长的嗥叫更要叫人打颤。它那么孤寂，那么凄凉。不管这声音是狼蹲在谁家木屋外等着吃残羹时发出的，还是它全力追捕猎物时发出的——那叫声一律野蛮、凶残，叫人毛骨悚然。
贝蒂从来没听见过。她虽然胆大，可当林中的嗥叫在小溪灌木丛那边得到另一声嗥叫的应和时，她把戴着小手套的手悄悄塞进韦策尔的臂弯，仰起脸，用惊惶的眼睛望着他。
不到半个钟头，嗥叫、吠声、长嚎汇成的齐鸣在寒气里可怖地高涨起来，越聚越多的狼群已经逼到雪橇后面不足一百码。它们飞奔的四爪踏在雪上的细碎声响清晰可闻。一条条瘦长的黑影步步逼近。很快，狼群近得雪橇上的人都看得见它们的眼睛了，亮得像一粒粒绿色的小火球。一头比同伴更胆大、显然是狼群头领的瘦狼，一路蹿到离最后一挂雪橇只剩几码的地方。它每蹿一下就张开大口短促地吠一声，仿佛在给手下壮胆。

===Original===

Almost simultaneously with the red flame that burst from Wetzel’s rifle came a sharp yelp of agony from the leader. He rolled over and over. Instantly followed a horrible mingling of snarls and barks, and snapping of jaws as the band fought over the body of their luckless comrade.
This short delay gave the advantage to the horses. When the wolves again appeared they were a long way behind. The distance to the fort was now short and the horses were urged to their utmost. The wolves kept up the chase until they reached the creek bridge and the mill. Then they slowed up: the howling became desultory, and finally the dark forms disappeared in the thickets.

===Chinese===

韦策尔的枪口喷出红光的几乎同一瞬，头狼发出一声凄厉的惨嗥。它接连翻滚着倒下。紧接着响起一阵令人毛骨悚然的咆哮、吠叫和利齿的撕咬——狼群在争夺它们那个倒霉同伴的尸首。
这短短的耽搁让马占了先。狼群再露面时，已被甩下老远。离堡的路已不远，马被催到了极限。狼群一路追到小溪桥和磨坊，才放慢了脚步：嗥声渐渐零落，末了，那些黑影终于消失在灌木丛里。

## VIII / 八
===Original===

Winter dragged by uneventfully for Betty. Unlike the other pioneer girls, who were kept busy all the time with their mending, and linsey weaving, and household duties, Betty had nothing to divert her but her embroidery and her reading. These she found very tiresome. Her maid was devoted to her and never left a thing undone. Annie was old Sam’s daughter, and she had waited on Betty since she had been a baby. The cleaning or mending or darning﻿—anything in the shape of work that would have helped pass away the monotonous hours for Betty, was always done before she could lift her hand.
During the day she passed hours in her little room and most of them were dreamed away by her window. Lydia and Alice came over sometimes and whiled away the tedious moments with their bright chatter and merry laughter, their castle-building, and their romancing on heroes and love and marriage as girls always will until the end of time. They had not forgotten Mr. Clarke, but as Betty had rebuked them with a dignity which forbade any further teasing on that score, they had transferred their fun-making to the use of Mr. Miller’s name.
Fearing her brothers’ wrath Betty had not told them of the scene with Miller at the dance. She had learned enough of rough border justice to dread the consequence of such a disclosure. She permitted Miller to come to the house, although she never saw him alone. Miller had accepted this favor gratefully. He said that on the night of the dance he had been a little the worse for Dan Watkins’s strong liquor, and that, together with his bitter disappointment, made him act in the mad way which had so grievously offended her. He exerted himself to win her forgiveness. Betty was always tenderhearted, and though she did not trust him, she said they might still be friends, but that that depended on his respect for her forbearance. Miller had promised he would never refer to the old subject and he had kept his word.
Indeed Betty welcomed any diversion for the long winter evenings. Occasionally some of the young people visited her, and they sang and danced, roasted apples, popped chestnuts, and played games. Often Wetzel and Major McColloch came in after supper. Betty would come down and sing for them, and afterward would coax Indian lore and woodcraft from Wetzel, or she would play checkers with the Major.

===Chinese===

冬天就这样波澜不惊地捱过去了，贝蒂的日子单调乏味。别的拓荒者家姑娘整天忙个不停，缝缝补补、织麻布、操持家务，贝蒂却除了刺绣和读书无事可做，而这两样她早已腻烦。她的女仆忠心耿耿，事事不肯漏下一件。安妮是老萨姆的女儿，打贝蒂还是襁褓婴儿时起就伺候她。打扫、缝补、织补——凡是能帮贝蒂打发沉闷时辰的活计，还没等她抬手，就总已被做好了。
白天她多半守在自己的小房间里，大半时光都在窗前恍惚做梦。莉迪亚和爱丽丝时常过来，用她们欢快的谈笑、她们的白日美梦、她们对英雄、爱情和婚姻的畅想，替她消磨沉闷的时刻——姑娘们历来如此，直到世界末日也不会改。她们没有忘记克拉克先生，只是贝蒂曾正颜厉色地申斥过她们，叫她们不敢再拿这事取笑，于是她们的打趣便转到了米勒先生身上。
贝蒂怕哥哥们动怒，没有把舞会上米勒发作的那一幕告诉他们。她见惯了边地粗野的私刑，深知这事一旦说破会招来什么后果。她仍容许米勒上门，只是从不同他单独见面。米勒对这份宽容感激不尽。他说舞会那晚自己多喝了点丹·沃特金斯的烈酒，再加上失望之痛，才做出那样疯癫的举动，大大得罪了她。他百般努力想赢得她的宽恕。贝蒂心肠终究是软的，尽管并不信任他，还是说他们仍可做朋友，但要看他是否尊重她的容忍。米勒答应再不提旧事，他也守住了诺言。
的确，漫长的冬夜里，任何消遣贝蒂都欢迎。偶尔有年轻人来串门，大家唱歌跳舞，烤苹果，爆栗子，做游戏。韦策尔和麦卡洛克少校也常在晚饭后过来。贝蒂会下楼为他们唱歌，然后缠着韦策尔讲印第安人的传说和林中的本事，或是陪少校下跳棋。

===Original===

If she succeeded in winning from him, which in truth was not often, she teased him unmercifully. When Col. Zane and the Major had settled down to their series of games, from which nothing short of Indians could have diverted them, Betty sat by Wetzel. The silent man of the woods, an appellation the hunter had earned by his reticence, talked for Betty as he would for no one else.
One night while Col. Zane, his wife and Betty were entertaining Capt. Boggs and Major McColloch and several of Betty’s girl friends, after the usual music and singing, storytelling became the order of the evening. Little Noah told of the time he had climbed the apple tree in the yard after a raccoon and got severely bitten.
“One day,” said Noah, “I heard Tige barking out in the orchard and I ran out there and saw a funny little fur ball up in the tree with a black tail and white rings around it. It looked like a pretty cat with a sharp nose. Every time Tige barked the little animal showed his teeth and swelled up his back. I wanted him for a pet. I got Sam to give me a sack and I climbed the tree and the nearer I got to him the farther he backed down the limb. I followed him and put out the sack to put it over his head and he bit me. I fell from the limb, but he fell too and Tige killed him and Sam stuffed him for me.”
“Noah, you are quite a valiant hunter,” said Betty. “Now, Jonathan, remember that you promised to tell me of your meeting with Daniel Boone.”
“It was over on the Muskingong near the mouth of the Sandusky. I was hunting in the open woods along the bank when I saw an Indian. He saw me at the same time and we both treed. There we stood a long time each afraid to change position. Finally I began to get tired and resorted to an old ruse. I put my coonskin cap on my ramrod and cautiously poked it from behind the tree, expecting every second to hear the whistle of the redskin’s bullet. Instead I heard a jolly voice yell: ‘Hey, young feller, you’ll have to try something better’n that.’ I looked and saw a white man standing out in the open and shaking all over with laughter. I went up to him and found him to be a big strong fellow with an honest, merry face. He said: ‘I’m Boone.’ I was considerably taken aback, especially when I saw he knew I was a white man all the time.

===Chinese===

若她侥幸赢了他一盘——其实难得有这种时候——便毫不留情地奚落他。等赞恩上校和少校沉入他们的连环棋局——这时除非印第安人来袭，什么也分不了他们的心——贝蒂就坐到韦策尔身旁。这个沉默的林中人——猎人是因他的寡言赢得这个称呼的——肯为贝蒂开口，换成别人他一个字也不多讲。
一天晚上，赞恩上校夫妇和贝蒂正款待博格斯上尉、麦卡洛克少校和贝蒂的几位女友，照例奏乐唱歌之后，讲故事成了当晚的余兴。小诺亚讲了他爬上院里苹果树捉浣熊、被狠狠咬了一口的事。
「有一天，」诺亚说，「我听见蒂格在果园里叫，就跑了出去，看见树上一个毛茸茸的小玩意儿，黑尾巴，一圈圈白环子。像只挺俊的猫，鼻子尖尖的。蒂格一叫，那小东西就龇牙，把背拱起来。我想捉它来养。我求萨姆给我一条口袋，就爬上树去。我越凑近，它越往枝子梢上退。我跟着它，伸出口袋要罩它的脑袋，它就咬了我一口。我从树枝上掉下来，它也掉了下来，蒂格咬死了它，萨姆替我把它做成了标本。」
「诺亚，你真称得上一个勇猛的猎人，」贝蒂说。「对了，乔纳森，可别忘了，你答应过给我讲你和丹尼尔·布恩相遇的事。」
「那是在马斯金格姆河边，快到桑达斯基河口的地方。我正沿着河岸在开阔的林子里打猎，看见一个印第安人。他也同时看见了我，我们两个各各贴上一棵树。我们就在那儿僵了半天，谁也不敢挪动。后来我渐渐乏了，使出一条老计策。我把浣熊皮帽子套在通条上，小心地从树后探出去，时时刻刻等着听见红人的枪弹呼啸而来。谁知却听见一个快活的声音大喊：『嘿，小老弟，你得换个花样儿才行。』我一看，一个白人站在开阔地里，笑得浑身直颤。我走上前去，见他是个高大结实的汉子，一张诚实快活的脸。他说：『我是布恩。』我吃了一惊，尤其看见他早知道我是白人。

===Original===

We camped and hunted along the river a week and at the Falls of the Muskingong he struck out for his Kentucky home.”
“Here is Wetzel,” said Col. Zane, who had risen and gone to the door. “Now, Betty, try and get Lew to tell us something.”
“Come, Lewis, here is a seat by me,” said Betty. “We have been pleasantly passing the time. We have had bear stories, snake stories, ghost stories﻿—all kinds of tales. Will you tell us one?”
“Lewis, did you ever have a chance to kill a hostile Indian and not take it?” asked Col. Zane.
“Never but once,” answered Lewis.
“Tell us about it. I imagine it will be interesting.”
“Well, I ain’t good at tellin’ things,” began Lewis. “I reckon I’ve seen some strange sights. I kin tell you about the only redskin I ever let off. Three years ago I was takin’ a fall hunt over on the Big Sandy, and I run into a party of Shawnees. I plugged a chief and started to run. There was some good runners and I couldn’t shake ’em in the open country. Comin’ to the Ohio I jumped in and swum across, keepin’ my rifle and powder dry by holdin’ ’em up. I hid in some bulrushes and waited. Pretty soon along comes three Injuns, and when they saw where I had taken to the water they stopped and held a short powwow. Then they all took to the water. This was what I was waitin’ for. When they got nearly acrosst I shot the first redskin, and loadin’ quick got a bullet into the others. The last Injun did not sink. I watched him go floatin’ down stream expectin’ every minute to see him go under as he was hurt so bad he could hardly keep his head above water. He floated down a long ways and the current carried him to a pile of driftwood which had lodged against a little island. I saw the Injun crawl up on the drift. I went down stream and by keepin’ the island between me and him I got out to where he was. I pulled my tomahawk and went around the head of the island and found the redskin leanin’ against a big log. He was a young brave and a fine lookin’ strong feller. He was tryin’ to stop the blood from my bullet-hole in his side. When he saw me he tried to get up, but he was too weak. He smiled, pointed to the wound and said: ‘Deathwind not heap times bad shot.’ Then he bowed his head and waited for the tomahawk. Well, I picked him up and carried him ashore and made a shack by a spring. I stayed there with him.

===Chinese===

我们在河边安营打了一个星期的猎，到了马斯金格姆瀑布那儿，他便动身回他在肯塔基的家去了。」
「韦策尔来了，」赞恩上校说，他已起身走到门口。「来，贝蒂，想法子让刘易斯给我们讲点什么。」
「来，刘易斯，我这儿有个座儿，」贝蒂说。「我们方才正开心地打发时光呢。熊的故事、蛇的故事、鬼的故事——各样的故事都讲遍了。你也给我们讲一个吧？」
「刘易斯，你可曾有过杀一个敌对印第安人的机会，却放过了？」赞恩上校问。
「只有一回，」刘易斯答道。
「讲给我们听听。我想一定有意思。」
「唉，俺不会讲故事，」刘易斯开了腔。「俺这辈子见的怪事倒不少。俺给你们说说俺唯一放走过的一个红人吧。三年前俺在大桑迪那边打秋猎，撞上一伙肖尼人。俺撂倒了一个酋长，撒腿就跑。他们里有几个跑得飞快，开阔地上俺甩不脱他们。到了俄亥俄河边，俺跳进水里游了过去，把枪和火药举在头顶上，没让沾水。俺藏进一片灯心草里等着。不大会儿来了三个印第安人，他们看见俺下水的地方，停下开了个小会。然后他们全都下了水。俺等的就是这个。等他们快游到对岸，俺一枪打中头一个，赶紧装药，又给其余的每人一粒弹。最后一个印第安人没有沉下去。俺看着他顺水往下漂，时时刻刻等着他沉底——他伤得那么重，脑袋勉强露出水面。他漂了老远，水流把他冲到一座小岛边上一堆搁浅的浮木上。俺看见那印第安人爬上了浮木。俺顺流而下，借着小岛挡住他的视线，绕到他跟前。俺拔出战斧，绕过岛头，看见那红人靠在一根大木头旁。他是个年轻的武士，模样俊俏，身板结实，正拼命按住俺那枪眼儿里往外淌的血。他一见俺就想撑起来，可他太虚弱了。他笑了笑，指指伤口，说：『死神风不是回回都打不准。』说完他低下头，等俺的斧子。唉，俺把他抱起来，背到岸上，在一眼泉水边搭了个窝棚。俺就在那儿守着他。

===Original===

When he got well enough to stand a few days’ travel I got him across the river and givin’ him a hunk of deer meat I told him to go, and if I ever saw him again I’d make a better shot.
“A year afterwards I trailed two Shawnees into Wingenund’s camp and got surrounded and captured. The Delaware chief is my great enemy. They beat me, shot salt into my legs, made me run the gauntlet, tied me on the back of a wild mustang. Then they got ready to burn me at the stake. That night they painted my face black and held the usual death dances. Some of the braves got drunk and worked themselves into a frenzy. I allowed I’d never see daylight. I seen that one of the braves left to guard me was the young feller I had wounded the year before. He never took no notice of me. In the gray of the early mornin’ when all were asleep and the other watch dozin’ I felt cold steel between my wrists and my buckskin thongs dropped off. Then my feet were cut loose. I looked round and in the dim light I seen my young brave. He handed me my own rifle, knife and tomahawk, put his finger on his lips and with a bright smile, as if to say he was square with me, he pointed to the east. I was out of sight in a minute.”
“How noble of him!” exclaimed Betty, her eyes all aglow. “He paid his debt to you, perhaps at the price of his life.”
“I have never known an Indian to forget a promise, or a kind action, or an injury,” observed Col. Zane.
“Are the Indians half as bad as they are called?” asked Betty. “I have heard as many stories of their nobility as of their cruelty.”
“The Indians consider that they have been robbed and driven from their homes. What we think hideously inhuman is war to them,” answered Col. Zane.
“When I came here from Fort Pitt I expected to see and fight Indians every day,” said Capt. Boggs. “I have been here at Wheeling for nearly two years and have never seen a hostile Indian. There have been some Indians in the vicinity during that time, but not one has shown himself to me. I’m not up to Indian tricks, I know, but I think the last siege must have been enough for them. I don’t believe we shall have any more trouble from them.”
“Captain,” called out Col. Zane, banging his hand on the table. “I’ll bet you my best horse to a keg of gunpowder that you see enough Indians before you are a year older to make you wish you had never seen or heard of the western border.”

===Chinese===

等他好些、经得起几天赶路了，俺送他过了河，割一块鹿肉给他，叫他走，还说俺要是再见到他，就打他个准的。
「一年之后，俺追踪两个肖尼人，追进了温根努德的营地，叫他们团团围住活捉了。那特拉华酋长是俺的死对头。他们打俺，往俺腿里打盐弹，逼俺跑夹道鞭笞，把俺绑在一匹野马的背上。后来他们又要架柴烧死俺。那天夜里他们把俺的脸涂黑，照例跳起死亡的舞蹈。有几个武士喝醉了，闹得发了狂。俺想俺再也见不着日头了。俺瞧见留下看守俺的武士里，有一个正是俺前一年打伤的那个后生。他只当不认得俺。天蒙蒙亮，众人都睡了，另一个看守也打着盹儿，俺忽觉手腕上一凉，鹿皮绑绳掉在了地上。接着脚上的绳子也割断了。俺四下一看，晨光熹微里正是俺那个年轻武士。他把俺自己的枪、刀和战斧递给俺，手指竖在嘴唇上，脸上亮亮地一笑，像是说两不相欠了，然后朝东边一指。俺一眨眼就没影儿了。」
「他多么高尚！」贝蒂喊道，眼睛闪闪发亮。「他报答了你，说不定是拿性命作抵的。」
「我从不曾见过印第安人忘掉一个许诺、一件恩情或一桩仇恨，」赞恩上校说。
「印第安人真有人们说的那么坏吗？」贝蒂问。「他们的高贵和他们的残忍，我听过的故事一样多。」
「印第安人认定自己受了抢掠，被赶出了家园。我们看来惨绝人寰的事，在他们就是战争，」赞恩上校答道。
「我从皮特堡来的时候，打算天天见着印第安人、天天打仗，」博格斯上尉说。「我来惠灵快两年了，一个敌对的印第安人也没见过。这期间左近也有印第安人出没，可没有一个在我眼前露过面。我知道我不懂印第安人的伎俩，可我想上回那次围困够他们受的了。我看他们不会再来找麻烦。」
「上尉，」赞恩上校喊道，一拳砸在桌上。「我拿我最好的马，跟你一桶火药打赌：不出一年，你见到的印第安人保管多得叫你但愿自己从没见过、也没听说过这西部边境。」

===Original===

“And I’ll go you the same bet,” said Major McColloch.
“You see, Captain, you must understand a little of the nature of the Indian,” continued Col. Zane. “We have had proof that the Delawares and the Shawnees have been preparing for an expedition for months. We shall have another siege some day and to my thinking it will be a longer and harder one than the last. What say you, Wetzel?”
“I ain’t sayin’ much, but I don’t calkilate on goin’ on any long hunts this summer,” answered the hunter.
“And do you think Tarhe, Wingenund, Pipe, Cornplanter, and all those chiefs will unite their forces and attack us?” asked Betty of Wetzel.
“Cornplanter won’t. He has been paid for most of his land and he ain’t so bitter. Tarhe is not likely to bother us. But Pipe and Wingenund and Red Fox﻿—they all want blood.”
“Have you seen these chiefs?” said Betty.
“Yes, I know ’em all and they all know me,” answered the hunter. “I’ve watched over many a trail waitin’ for one of ’em. If I can ever get a shot at any of ’em I’ll give up Injuns and go farmin’. Good night, Betty.”
“What a strange man is Wetzel,” mused Betty, after the visitors had gone. “Do you know, Eb, he is not at all like anyone else. I have seen the girls shudder at the mention of his name and I have heard them say they could not look in his eyes. He does not affect me that way. It is not often I can get him to talk, but sometimes he tells me beautiful things about the woods; how he lives in the wilderness, his home under the great trees; how every leaf on the trees and every blade of grass has its joy for him as well as its knowledge; how he curls up in his little bark shack and is lulled to sleep by the sighing of the wind through the pine tops. He told me he has often watched the stars for hours at a time. I know there is a waterfall back in the Black Forest somewhere that Lewis goes to, simply to sit and watch the water tumble over the precipice.”
“Wetzel is a wonderful character, even to those who know him only as an Indian slayer and a man who wants no other occupation. Someday he will go off on one of these long jaunts and will never return. That is certain. The day is fast approaching when a man like Wetzel will be of no use in life. Now, he is a necessity. Like Tige he can smell Indians. Betty, I believe Lewis tells you so much and is so kind and gentle toward you because he cares for you.”

===Chinese===

「这赌我也跟一份，」麦卡洛克少校说。
「你瞧，上尉，你得懂得一点印第安人的禀性，」赞恩上校接着说。「已经有凭据，特拉华人和肖尼人一连几个月在筹备一场出征。早晚还有一场围困，依我看，那要比上一场更久、更难对付。你说呢，韦策尔？」
「俺话不多，不过俺今年夏天不打算出远门打猎，」猎人答道。
「你看塔赫、温根努德、派普、康普兰特这些酋长，会合兵一处来打我们吗？」贝蒂问韦策尔。
「康普兰特不会。他的地多半得了钱，他没那么大火气。塔赫多半不来搅扰我们。可派普、温根努德，还有红狐——他们都想要血。」
「你见过这些酋长吗？」贝蒂说。
「见过，俺认得他们，他们也个个认得俺，」猎人答道。「多少回俺守在小径上，等他们哪一个。俺要是能碰上他们哪一个给他一枪，俺就此不干这行，回家种地去。晚安，贝蒂。」
「韦策尔这人真奇怪，」客人走后，贝蒂喃喃自语。「你可知道，埃布，他跟谁都两样。我见过姑娘们一听他的名字就发抖，也听她们说不敢看他的眼睛。他给我的感觉却不是那样。我不常能引他开口，可他有时跟我讲树林里顶美的光景：他怎样住在荒野里，家就安在大树底下；树上的每片叶子、地上的每根草，怎样于他又是一份欢喜又是一份学问；他怎样蜷在他小小的树皮棚里，听着风穿过松顶的呜呜声入睡。他告诉我，他常一连几个钟头看星星。我知道黑森林深处有一挂瀑布，刘易斯常去，就为坐在那儿看水从崖上跌下去。」
「韦策尔是个了不起的人物，哪怕只把他当个印第安人杀手、当个不想要别的营生的人看，也是如此。早晚有一天他又要出这样一趟远门，一去不回。这是定的。韦策尔这样的人在世上派用场的日子快到了。如今呢，他少不了。他像蒂格一样闻得出印第安人。贝蒂，我信刘易斯肯对你说这么多、对你这样温存和气，是因为他心里有你。」

===Original===

“Of course Lew likes me. I know he does and I want him to,” said Betty. “But he does not care as you seem to think. Grandmother Watkins said the same. I am sure both of you are wrong.”
“Did Dan’s mother tell you that? Well, she’s pretty shrewd. It’s quite likely, Betty, quite likely. It seems to me you are not so quick witted as you used to be.”
“Why so?” asked Betty, quickly.
“Well, you used to be different somehow,” said her brother, as he patted her hand.
“Do you mean I am more thoughtful?”
“Yes, and sometimes you seem sad.”
“I have tried to be brave and﻿—and happy,” said Betty, her voice trembling slightly.
“Yes, yes, I know you have, Betty. You have done wonderfully well here in this dead place. But tell me, don’t be angry, don’t you think too much of someone?”
“You have no right to ask me that,” said Betty, flushing and turning away toward the stairway.
“Well, well, child, don’t mind me. I did not mean anything. There, good night, Betty.”
Long after she had gone upstairs Col. Zane sat by his fireside. From time to time he sighed. He thought of the old Virginia home and of the smile of his mother. It seemed only a few short years since he had promised her that he would take care of the baby sister. How had he kept that promise, made when Betty was a little thing bouncing on his knee? It seemed only yesterday. How swift the flight of time! Already Betty was a woman; her sweet, gay girlhood had passed; already a shadow had fallen on her face, the shadow of a secret sorrow.
---
March with its blustering winds had departed, and now April’s showers and sunshine were gladdening the hearts of the settlers. Patches of green freshened the slopes of the hills; the lilac bushes showed tiny leaves, and the maple buds were bursting. Yesterday a bluebird﻿—surest harbinger of spring﻿—had alighted on the fencepost and had sung his plaintive song. A few more days and the blossoms were out mingling their pink and white with the green; the redbud, the hawthorne, and the dogwood were in bloom, checkering the hillsides.
“Bessie, spring is here,” said Col. Zane, as he stood in the doorway. “The air is fresh, the sun shines warm, the birds are singing; it makes me feel good.”
“Yes, it is pleasant to have spring with us again,” answered his wife. “I think, though, that in winter I am happier. In summer I am always worried.

「刘易斯当然喜欢我。我知道他喜欢，我也愿意他喜欢，」贝蒂说。「可他不是你们想的那样。沃特金斯老太太也这么说。我肯定你们俩都错了。」
「丹的母亲这么跟你说的？嗯，她眼力不差。十有八九是这样，贝蒂，十有八九。要叫我说，你可不比从前那么机灵了。」
「怎么见得？」贝蒂忙问。
「嗯，你从前不知怎么不是这样，」她哥哥说着，拍了拍她的手。
「你是说我心思重了？」
「是啊，而且你有时候好像不快活。」
「我一直想装得勇敢，装得——快活，」贝蒂说，声音微微发颤。
「是了，是了，我知道你尽力了，贝蒂。你在这死气沉沉的地方，已经做得再好不过。可是告诉我——别恼——你是不是把一个人想得太重了？」
「你没有权利问我这个，」贝蒂说着涨红了脸，转身朝楼梯走去。
「好了，好了，孩子，别理会我。我没别的意思。好了，晚安，贝蒂。」
她上楼去后许久，赞恩上校还坐在炉边，不时叹口气。他想起弗吉尼亚的老家，想起母亲的笑容。仿佛只是几年前的事，他向母亲许诺要照看这小妹妹。那时贝蒂还是个在他膝上蹦跳的小不点儿——这个诺言，他守得怎么样？仿佛就在昨天。光阴过得真快！贝蒂转眼已是大人，她那甜美快活的少女时光过去了，脸上已落上一层暗影，一桩隐秘的伤心事的暗影。
---
三月带着呼啸的狂风走了，如今四月的阵雨和阳光，又让拓荒者们心头欢喜。山坡上一片片新绿；紫丁香抽出小小的叶子，枫树的芽苞绽开了。昨天，一只蓝知更鸟——春天最可靠的信使——落在栅栏桩上，唱起它哀婉的歌。再过几天，花儿开了，粉的白的掺进绿意里；紫荆、山楂、山茱萸都开了花，把山坡织成一方方锦绣。
「贝茜，春天来了，」赞恩上校站在门口说。「空气新鲜，太阳晒得暖，鸟儿在唱，我这心里痛快。」
「是啊，春天回来是叫人高兴，」他妻子答道。「不过依我说，冬天我倒更快活。一到夏天我总提心吊胆的。

I am afraid for the children to be out of my sight, and when you are away on a hunt I am distraught until you are home safe.”
“Well, if the redskins let us alone this summer it will be something new,” he said, laughing. “By the way, Bess, some new people came to the fort last night. They rafted down from the Monongahela settlements. Some of the women suffered considerably. I intend to offer them the cabin on the hill until they can cut the timber and run up a house. Sam said the cabin roof leaked and the chimney smoked, but with a little work I think they can be made more comfortable there than at the blockhouse.”
“It is the only vacant cabin in the settlement. I can accommodate the women folks here.”
“Well, we’ll see about it. I don’t want you and Betty inconvenienced. I’ll send Sam up to the cabin and have him fix things up a bit and make it more habitable.”
The door opened, admitting Col. Zane’s elder boy. The lad’s face was dirty, his nose was all bloody, and a big bruise showed over his right eye.
“For the land’s sake!” exclaimed his mother. “Look at the boy. Noah, come here. What have you been doing?”
Noah crept close to his mother and grasping her apron with both hands hid his face. Mrs. Zane turned the boy around and wiped his discolored features with a wet towel. She gave him a little shake and said: “Noah, have you been fighting again?”
“Let him go and I’ll tell you about it,” said the Colonel, and when the youngster had disappeared he continued: “Right after breakfast Noah went with me down to the mill. I noticed several children playing in front of Reihart’s blacksmith shop. I went in, leaving Noah outside. I got the plowshare which I had left with Reihart to be repaired. He came to the door with me and all at once he said: ‘Look at the kids.’ I looked and saw Noah walk up to a boy and say something to him. The lad was a stranger, and I have no doubt belongs to these new people I told you about. He was bigger than Noah. At first the older boy appeared very friendly and evidently wanted to join the others in their game. I guess Noah did not approve of this, for after he had looked the stranger over he hauled away and punched the lad soundly. To make it short the strange boy gave Noah the worst beating he ever got in his life. I told Noah to come straight to you and confess.”
“Well, did you ever!” ejaculated Mrs. Zane. “Noah is a bad boy.

孩子们一离了我的眼我就怕；你出门打猎，我不安神，直到你平平安安回了家。」
「嗯，今年夏天红人要是肯放过我们，那可就新鲜了，」他笑着说。「对了，贝丝，昨晚堡里来了几户新人家，是从莫农加希拉的移民点放排下来的。有几个女人吃了不少苦。我打算把山上那间小屋借给他们住，等他们砍了木料、盖起房子再说。萨姆说那屋顶漏雨，烟囱倒烟，不过收拾收拾，总比住棱堡里舒坦。」
「那是移民点上唯一空着的小屋了。女眷们我可以安置在这儿。」
「嗯，再看吧。我不想叫你和贝蒂受委屈。我打发萨姆上去把屋子拾掇拾掇，先住得人才行。」
门开了，进来的是赞恩上校家的大儿子。孩子脸上脏兮兮的，鼻子流着血，右眼上头乌青了一大块。
「老天爷！」他母亲嚷道。「瞧瞧这孩子。诺亚，过来。你干什么来着？」
诺亚蹭到母亲跟前，两只手抓住她的围裙，把脸埋了起来。赞恩太太把孩子转过来，用湿毛巾擦他那张花了的脸，又轻轻摇了摇他，说：「诺亚，又打架了？」
「放开他，我说给你听，」上校说。等那小家伙溜走了，他接着讲：「早饭后诺亚跟我到磨坊去。我看见几个孩子在赖哈特铁匠铺门前玩。我进去取我送去修的犁头，把诺亚留在外头。赖哈特送我到门口，忽然说：『瞧那些孩子。』我一瞧，诺亚走到一个男孩跟前，说了句什么。那孩子面生，不用说是那几户新来人家的。他比诺亚个头大。起先那大孩子显得挺和气，分明想加入他们一起玩。我猜诺亚不乐意，他把那生人上下打量了一番，抡起拳头结结实实给了那孩子一下。长话短说，那陌生的男孩把诺亚揍了个透，他这辈子没挨过那样的打。我叫诺亚直接回来向你认错。」
「嗬，真是稀奇！」赞恩太太脱口说道。「诺亚真是个坏孩子。

===Original===

And you stood and watched him fight. You are laughing about it now. Ebenezer Zane, I would not put it beneath you to set Noah to fighting. I know you used to make the little niggers fight. Anyway, it serves Noah right and I hope it will be a lesson to him.”
“I’ll make you a bet, Bessie,” said the Colonel, with another laugh. “I’ll bet you that unless we lock him up, Noah will fight that boy every day or every time he meets him.”
“I won’t bet,” said Mrs. Zane, with a smile of resignation.
“Where’s Betts? I haven’t seen her this morning. I am going over to Short Creek tomorrow or next day, and think I’ll take her with me. You know I am to get a commission to lay out several settlements along the river, and I want to get some work finished at Short Creek this spring. Mrs. Raymer will be delighted to have Betty. Shall I take her?”
“By all means. A visit there will brighten her up and do her good.”
“Well, what on earth have you been doing?” cried the Colonel. His remark had been called forth by a charming vision that had entered by the open door. Betty﻿—for it was she﻿—wore a little red cap set jauntily on her black hair. Her linsey dress was crumpled and covered with hayseed.
“I’ve been in the haymow,” said Betty, waving a small basket. “For a week that old black hen has circumvented me, but at last I have conquered. I found the nest in the farthest corner under the hay.”
“How did you get up in the loft?” inquired Mrs. Zane.
“Bessie, I climbed up the ladder of course. I acknowledge being unusually lighthearted and happy this morning, but I have not as yet grown wings. Sam said I could not climb up that straight ladder, but I found it easy enough.”
“You should not climb up into the loft,” said Mrs. Zane, in a severe tone. “Only last fall Hugh Bennet’s little boy slid off the hay down into one of the stalls and the horse kicked him nearly to death.”
“Oh, fiddlesticks, Bessie, I am not a baby,” said Betty, with vehemence. “There is not a horse in the barn but would stand on his hind legs before he would step on me, let alone kick me.”
“I don’t know, Betty, but I think that black horse Mr. Clarke left here would kick anyone,” remarked the Colonel.
“Oh, no, he would not hurt me.”
“Betty, we have had pleasant weather for about three days,” said the Colonel, gravely. “In that time you have let out that crazy bear of yours to turn everything topsy-turvy.
