大路远处，离堡约五百码的地方，亮起一个光点。起初不动，随后怪模怪样地一颠一颠，忽左忽右，忽上忽下，像个鬼火灯笼。
「搞什么名堂？」赞恩上校喃喃道，大惑不解。「杰克，怪了，它越来越大。」
千真万确，那点火——不管是什么——越变越大。赞恩上校想，兴许是骑马的人打的灯。可要是这样，马蹄声呢？这样的石头崖上，没有马能跑得没声。不会是马。守望的人给这新出的一幕迷住了又揪着心，它像是不祥之兆；他们带着只有惯历危险的人才有的耐心等着。他们知道，不管那是什么，总是野人什么恶毒的诡计，而且来得不会慢了。
那光在路上一路之字形地窜来窜去，飞快地朝堡子逼近。那动态，像黑夜里一只提灯大幅地荡。又捱了一刻叫人屏息的工夫，光后头现出一个印第安武士精瘦的身影。他顺着大路朝堡子的方向，跑得快得几乎叫人不能信。他从栅栏前不足七十五码的地方全速掠过，一箭射出。那支箭像一条火蛇凌空飞去，姿态曼妙，越过棱堡，狠狠咚的一声，钉在后面一座小屋的屋顶上。迎着他齐发的枪弹，这大胆的武士毫发无损，一闪便没了影。
这样的事，最投野人的心。做成一件事，武士便成了勇士；为这份荣耀，哪个印第安人都肯一遭又一遭拿命去换。迎着这一手响起的欢呼呐喊，宣告了它的成功。
风早把那闷燃的箭扇成了明火，干透的屋顶着了，烧得凶猛。
「那个可恶的红人还要来，」乔纳森脱口道。
果然。那同样的一个小亮点又顺着大路来了，一秒快似一秒。

===Original===

No doubt the same Indian, emboldened by his success, and maddened with that thirst for glory so often fatal to his kind, was again making the effort to fire the blockhouse.
The eyes of Col. Zane and his companions were fastened on the light as it came nearer and nearer with its changing motion. The burning cabin brightened the square before the Fort. The slender, shadowy figure of the Indian could be plainly seen emerging from the gloom. So swiftly did he run that he seemed to have wings. Now he was in the full glare of the light. What a magnificent nerve, what a terrible assurance there was in his action! It seemed to paralyze all. The red arrow emitted a shower of sparks as it was discharged. This time it winged its way straight and true and imbedded itself in the roof of the blockhouse.
Almost at the same instant a solitary rifle shot rang out and the daring warrior plunged headlong, sliding face downward in the dust of the road, while from the Fort came that demoniac yell now grown so familiar.
“Wetzel’s compliments,” muttered Jonathan. “But the mischief is done. Look at that damned burning arrow. If it doesn’t blow out the Fort will go.”
The arrow was visible, but it seemed a mere spark. It alternately paled and glowed. One moment it almost went out, and the next it gleamed brightly. To the men, compelled to look on and powerless to prevent the burning of the now apparently doomed blockhouse, that spark was like the eye of Hell.
“Ho, the Fort,” yelled Col. Zane with all the power of his strong lungs. “Ho, Silas, the roof is on fire!”
Pandemonium had now broken out among the Indians. They could be plainly seen in the red glare thrown by the burning cabin. It had been a very dry season, the rough shingles were like tinder, and the inflammable material burst quickly into great flames, lighting up the valley as far as the edge of the forest. It was an awe-inspiring and a horrible spectacle. Columns of yellow and black smoke rolled heavenward; every object seemed dyed a deep crimson; the trees assumed fantastic shapes; the river veiled itself under a red glow. Above the roaring and crackling of the flames rose the inhuman yelling of the savages. Like demons of the inferno they ran to and fro, their naked painted bodies shining in the glare. One group of savages formed a circle and danced hands-around a stump as gayly as a band of schoolgirls at a May party.

===Chinese===

不用问，还是那个印第安人，得手壮了胆，又被那种往往送掉他同胞性命的求荣的渴火烧着，再来放火烧棱堡了。
赞恩上校和同伴们的眼睛盯住那团光，看它一颠一簸越逼越近。燃烧的小屋照亮了堡前的草坪。那印第安人瘦长幽暗的身影清清楚楚地从暗处显出来。他跑得那样快，简直像生了翅膀。这会儿他全在亮处了。多硬的胆，多吓人的笃定！这架势把人都镇住了。红色的箭离弦时迸出一蓬火星。这一回它又直又准，射进棱堡的屋顶。
几乎同时，孤零零一声步枪响，那大胆的勇士一头栽倒，脸冲下滑进大路的尘土里；堡子里传出那声如今人人听熟了的恶鬼般的呐喊。
「韦策尔敬上，」乔纳森咕哝道。「可是祸已经闯下了。瞧那支该死的火箭。要是吹不灭，堡子就完了。」
那箭看得见，却只是一粒火星，忽明忽暗。这一会儿险些熄了，下一会儿又亮起来。在只能眼睁睁看着、拦不住这眼见要完的棱堡烧掉的人眼里，那粒火星就是地狱的眼睛。
「喂，堡里的！」赞恩上校扯开雄浑的嗓门喊。「喂，赛拉斯，屋顶着火了！」
印第安人中间这时炸了锅。燃烧的小屋红光一照，他们看得清清楚楚。天旱了很久，粗糙的木瓦像引火柴，见火就着，腾起大团火焰，把河谷照到林边。那是又威严又可怕的景象。黄的黑的烟柱滚滚上天；万物都像染上一层深红；树现出奇形怪状；河罩在一层红光底下。火焰的吼声爆响之上，浮着野人非人的呐喊。他们像地狱里的恶鬼跑来跑去，赤裸的、涂彩的身子在红光里发亮。有一群野人围成一圈，绕着一截树桩转圈跳，快活得像一群五月节上的女学生。

===Original===

They wrestled with and hugged one another; they hopped, skipped and jumped, and in every possible way manifested their fiendish joy.
The British took no part in this revelry. To their credit it must be said they kept in the background as though ashamed of this horrible fire-war on people of their own blood.
“Why don’t they fire the cannon?” impatiently said Col. Zane. “Why don’t they do something?”
“Perhaps it is disabled, or maybe they are short of ammunition,” suggested Jonathan.
“The blockhouse will burn down before our eyes. Look! The hellhounds have set fire to the fence. I see men running and throwing water.”
“I see something on the roof of the blockhouse,” cried Jonathan. “There, down towards the east end of the roof and in the shadow of the chimney. And as I’m a living sinner it’s a man crawling towards that blazing arrow. The Indians have not discovered him yet. He is still in the shadow. But they’ll see him. God! What a nervy thing to do in the face of all those redskins. It is almost certain death!”
“Yes, and they see him,” said the Colonel.
With shrill yells the Indians bounded forward and aimed and fired their rifles at the crouching figure of the man. Some hid behind the logs they had rolled toward the Fort; others boldly faced the steady fire now pouring from the portholes. The savages saw in the movement of that man an attempt to defeat their long-cherished hope of burning the Fort. Seeing he was discovered, the man did not hesitate, nor did he lose a second. Swiftly he jumped and ran toward the end of the roof where the burning arrow, now surrounded by blazing shingles, was sticking in the roof. How he ever ran along that slanting roof and with a pail in his hand was incomprehensible. In moments like that men become superhuman. It all happened in an instant. He reached the arrow, kicked it over the wall, and then dashed the bucket of water on the blazing shingles. In that single instant, wherein his tall form was outlined against the bright light behind him, he presented the fairest kind of a mark for the Indians. Scores of rifles were leveled and discharged at him. The bullets pattered like hail on the roof of the blockhouse, but apparently none found their mark, for the man ran back and disappeared.
“It was Clarke!” exclaimed Col. Zane. “No one but Clarke has such light hair. Wasn’t that a plucky thing?”

===Chinese===

他们扭作一团，抱作一团；蹦啊，跳啊，窜啊，千方百计撒他们的鬼欢。
英国人没有捼和这场狂欢。凭良心说，他们退在一边，像是羞于这样对同种同血的同胞打这场火战。
「他们怎么不开炮？」赞恩上校焦躁地说。「怎么不干点什么？」
「兴许炮坏了，要不就是缺弹药，」乔纳森猜。
「棱堡要当着我们的面烧塌了。瞧！这群地狱的猎狗把栅栏点着了。我看见有人在跑着泼水。」
「棱堡顶上有东西，」乔纳森喊。「那儿，屋顶靠东头，烟囱影子里。我敢赌咒，是个人在朝那支烧着的箭爬。印第安人还没看见他。他还在影子里。可他们快看见他了。天哪！当着这一大片红人的面，这要多大的胆子。十有八九是死！」
「可不是，他们看见他了，」上校说。
印第安人尖声呐喊着扑上前，朝那蜷伏的人影瞄准开枪。有的躲在他们朝堡子滚来的木头后面，有的索性迎着枪眼里倾出的沉稳火力硬上。野人从那人的举动里看出，这是要断送他们烧堡的宿愿。那人见已被发现，一点也不迟疑，一秒也不耽搁。他纵身便起，朝屋顶那头奔去——烧着的箭钉在那里，四下的木瓦已经着了。他怎么能在那斜屋顶上跑，手里还提着桶，简直没法想。那样的时刻，人都成了超人。一切都发生在一瞬。他奔到箭前，一脚把它踢下墙去，又把一桶水泼在烧着的木瓦上。就在那一瞬，他高大的身影衬在身后的火光里，成了印第安人千载难逢的靶子。几十支枪一齐瞄准他放。子弹冰雹似的打在棱堡顶上，却像是一颗也没捱着他，那人跑回去，不见了。
「是克拉克！」赞恩上校喊。「除了克拉克，谁有这样的浅色头发。多了不起的胆量？」

===Original===

“It has saved the blockhouse for tonight,” answered Jonathan. “See, the Indians are falling back. They can’t stand in the face of that shooting. Hurrah! Look at them fall! It could not have happened better. The light from the cabin will prevent any more close attacks for an hour and daylight is near.”

===Chinese===

「这一下保住了棱堡过夜，」乔纳森答。「瞧，印第安人在退了。这样的枪口他们站不住。好哇！瞧他们倒下的样儿！再好不过了。小屋的光，一个钟头里能防住贴身的来犯，天也快亮了。」

## XIV / 十四
===Original===

The sun rose red. Its ruddy rays peeped over the eastern hills, kissed the treetops, glinted along the stony bluffs, and chased away the gloom of night from the valley. Its warm gleams penetrated the portholes of the Fort and cast long bright shadows on the walls; but it brought little cheer to the sleepless and almost exhausted defenders. It brought to many of the settlers the familiar old sailor’s maxim: “Redness ’a the morning, sailor’s warning.” Rising in its crimson glory the sun flooded the valley, dyeing the river, the leaves, the grass, the stones, tingeing everything with that awful color which stained the stairs, the benches, the floor, even the portholes of the blockhouse.
Historians call this the time that tried men’s souls. If it tried the men think what it must have been to those grand, heroic women. Though they had helped the men load and fire nearly forty-eight hours; though they had worked without a moment’s rest and were now ready to succumb to exhaustion; though the long room was full of stifling smoke and the sickening odor of burned wood and powder, and though the row of silent, covered bodies had steadily lengthened, the thought of giving up never occurred to the women. Death there would be sweet compared to what it would be at the hands of the redmen.
At sunrise Silas Zane, bare-chested, his face dark and fierce, strode into the bastion which was connected with the blockhouse. It was a small shedlike room, and with portholes opening to the river and the forest. This bastion had seen the severest fighting. Five men had been killed here. As Silas entered four haggard and powder-begrimed men, who were kneeling before the portholes, looked up at him. A dead man lay in one corner.
“Smith’s dead. That makes fifteen,” said Silas. “Fifteen out of forty-two, that leaves twenty-seven. We must hold out. Men, don’t expose yourselves recklessly. How goes it at the south bastion?”
“All right. There’s been firin’ over there all night,” answered one of the men. “I guess it’s been kinder warm over that way. But I ain’t heard any shootin’ for some time.”
“Young Bennet is over there, and if the men needed anything they would send him for it,” answered Silas. “I’ll send some food and water. Anything else?”
“Powder. We’re nigh out of powder,” replied the man addressed. “And we might jes as well make ready fer a high old time.

===Chinese===

太阳红着脸升起来。红彤彤的光线探过东边的山头，亲吻树梢，顺着石崖闪闪掃过，把夜的阴沉从河谷里赶走。它暖融融的光照进堡子的枪眼，在墙上投下长长的亮影；可是它带不给他们多少宽慰——守卫的人们彻夜无眠，几乎精疲力竭。它带给许多拓荒者的，倒是水手们那句老话：「早晨天红，水手当心。」太阳带着血色的辉煌升上来，照满河谷，染红河水、树叶、青草、石头，把一切都浸上那种可怕的颜色——那颜色也早已染了棱堡的楼梯、长凳、地板，连枪眼上都是。
史家称这个时期为考验人们灵魂的时期。它考验男人，想想它对那些高贵英勇的女人又是什么。她们帮着男人装弹开枪，几乎四十八个钟头了；她们没有一刻歇息，如今几乎要累垮；长屋里满是呛人的浓烟、烧焦的木头和火药的恶心气味；那一排蒙着布的静静的尸体，一直在加长——可是投降的念头，从来不曾在这些女人心里出现过。死在这里，比起落在红人手里的死法，还是甜的。
日出时，赛拉斯·赞恩袒着胸，脸色又黑又凶，大步走进连接棱堡的碉楼。那是一间小棚似的屋子，枪眼朝着河和森林。这座碉楼打得最苦，已经死了五个人。赛拉斯一进来，跪在枪眼前的四个满面烟灰的憔悴男人抬起头看他。墙角躺着一具尸体。
「史密斯死了。这就十五个了，」赛拉斯说。「四十二个人折了十五，剩二十七。我们一定要顶住。弟兄们，别拿命乱冒。南碉楼那边怎么样？」
「还行。那边一整夜都在打，」其中一人答。「我看那边打得够热的。可这阵子没听见什么枪声了。」
「小班纳特在那边，弟兄们缺什么会打发他来拿，」赛拉斯答。「我这就送吃的和水过去。还有别的没有？」
「火药。火药快见底了，」那人答。「咱们不妨备着点，好生热闹一场。

===Original===

The red devils hain’t been quiet all this last hour fer nothin’.”
Silas passed along the narrow hallway which led from the bastion into the main room of the blockhouse. As he turned the corner at the head of the stairway he encountered a boy who was dragging himself up the steps.
“Hello! Who’s this? Why, Harry!” exclaimed Silas, grasping the boy and drawing him into the room. Once in the light Silas saw that the lad was so weak he could hardly stand. He was covered with blood. It dripped from a bandage wound tightly about his arm; it oozed through a hole in his hunting shirt, and it flowed from a wound over his temple. The shadow of death was already stealing over the pallid face, but from the grey eyes shone an indomitable spirit, a spirit which nothing but death could quench.
“Quick!” the lad panted. “Send men to the south wall. The redskins are breakin’ in where the water from the spring runs under the fence.”
“Where are Metzar and the other men?”
“Dead! Killed last night. I’ve been there alone all night. I kept on shootin’. Then I gets plugged here under the chin. Knowin’ it’s all up with me I deserted my post when I heard the Injuns choppin’ on the fence where it was on fire last night. But I only﻿—run﻿—because﻿—they’re gettin’ in.”
“Wetzel, Bennet, Clarke!” yelled Silas, as he laid the boy on the bench.
Almost as Silas spoke the tall form of the hunter confronted him. Clarke and the other men were almost as prompt.
“Wetzel, run to the south wall. The Indians are cutting a hole through the fence.”
Wetzel turned, grabbed his rifle and an axe and was gone like a flash.
“Sullivan, you handle the men here. Bessie, do what you can for this brave lad. Come, Bennet, Clarke, we must follow Wetzel,” commanded Silas.
Mrs. Zane hastened to the side of the fainting lad. She washed away the blood from the wound over his temple. She saw that a bullet had glanced on the bone and that the wound was not deep or dangerous. She unlaced the hunting shirt at the neck and pulled the flaps apart. There on the right breast, on a line with the apex of the lung, was a horrible gaping wound. A murderous British slug had passed through the lad. From the hole at every heartbeat poured the dark, crimson lifetide. Mrs. Zane turned her white face away for a second; then she folded a small piece of linen, pressed it tightly over the wound, and wrapped a towel round the lad’s breast.

===Chinese===

这帮红鬼子这最后一个钟头不闹腾，可不是白不闹的。」
赛拉斯顺着从碉楼通向棱堡正屋的窄过道走过去。拐过楼梯口的墙角，迎面撞上一个孩子，正拖着步子往台阶上挪。
「喂！这是谁？哎呀，哈里！」赛拉斯喊道，一把抓住孩子，把他拉进屋里。到了亮处，赛拉斯才看清，这孩子虚弱得几乎站不住。他浑身是血。血从紧紧缠在胳膊上的绷带里滴下来，从猎衣的一个洞里渗出来，还从太阳穴上的伤口里淌出来。死亡的阴影已经悄悄爬上那张苍白的脸，可那双灰眼睛里，闪着一股不屈的神气，一股除了死亡什么都扑不灭的神气。
「快！」孩子喘着说。「派人去南墙。泉水从栅栏底下流进去的地方，红鬼子正在往里挖。」
「梅茨他们那几个人呢？」
「死了！昨晚上叫人杀了。我一人守了一夜。我不住地打。后来下巴颏底下叫人打了个洞。我知道自己完了，听见印第安人在砍栅栏——就是昨夜烧着的那段——我才离了哨位。我这是——跑回来——因为——他们要进来了。」
「韦策尔！班纳特！克拉克！」赛拉斯一面把孩子放倒在长凳上，一面喊。
话音没落，猎人高大的身影已经到了他跟前。克拉克和其他人也几乎一样快。
「韦策尔，快去南墙。印第安人在栅栏上砍洞。」
韦策尔一转身，抓起步枪和一柄斧子，一阵风去了。
「沙利文，这儿的人归你调度。贝茜，尽力照看这个勇敢的孩子。来，班纳特，克拉克，我们跟上韦策尔，」赛拉斯命令道。
赞恩太太急忙奔到这快要昏厥的孩子身边，洗净他太阳穴上的血。她看出一颗子弹在骨头上擦了一下，伤口不深，不碍事。她解开他领口的猎衣，把衣襟分开。右胸上，正对着肺尖的地方，是一个可怕的大洞。一颗歹毒的英国大号弹丸把孩子打了个穿。随着每一次心跳，暗红的生命之潮从洞口涌出来。赞恩太太把发白的脸扭开了一瞬；随后她折起一小块亚麻布，紧紧按在伤口上，又拿一条毛巾裹住孩子的胸膛。

===Original===

“Don’t waste time on me. It’s all over,” he whispered. “Will you call Betty here a minute?”
Betty came, white-faced and horror-stricken. For forty hours she had been living in a maze of terror. Her movements had almost become mechanical. She had almost ceased to hear and feel. But the light in the eyes of this dying boy brought her back to the horrible reality of the present.
“Oh, Harry! Harry! Harry!” was all Betty could whisper.
“I’m goin’, Betty. And I wanted﻿—you to say a little prayer for me﻿—and say goodbye to me,” he panted.
Betty knelt by the bench and tried to pray.
“I hated to run, Betty, but I waited and waited and nobody came, and the Injuns was getting’ in. They’ll find dead Injuns in piles out there. I was shootin’ fer you, Betty, and every time I aimed I thought of you.”
The lad rambled on, his voice growing weaker and weaker and finally ceasing. The hand which had clasped Betty’s so closely loosened its hold. His eyes closed. Betty thought he was dead, but no! he still breathed. Suddenly his eyes opened. The shadow of pain was gone. In its place shone a beautiful radiance.
“Betty, I’ve cared a lot for you﻿—and I’m dyin’﻿—happy because I’ve fought fer you﻿—and somethin’ tells me﻿—you’ll﻿—be saved. Goodbye.” A smile transformed his face and his gray eyes gazed steadily into hers. Then his head fell back. With a sigh his brave spirit fled.
Hugh Bennet looked once at the pale face of his son, then he ran down the stairs after Silas and Clarke. When the three men emerged from behind Capt. Boggs’s cabin, which was adjacent to the blockhouse, and which hid the south wall from their view, they were two hundred feet from Wetzel. They heard the heavy thump of a log being rammed against the fence; then a splitting and splintering of one of the six-inch oak planks. Another and another smashing blow and the lower half of one of the planks fell inwards, leaving an aperture large enough to admit an Indian. The men dashed forward to the assistance of Wetzel, who stood by the hole with upraised axe. At the same moment a shot rang out. Bennet stumbled and fell headlong. An Indian had shot through the hole in the fence. Silas and Alfred sheered off toward the fence, out of line. When within twenty yards of Wetzel they saw a swarthy-faced and athletic savage squeeze through the narrow crevice.

===Chinese===

「别在我身上费工夫了。我不中了，」他悄声说。「能叫贝蒂来待一会儿吗？」
贝蒂来了，脸白得吓人。四十个钟头以来，她一直活在一片恐怖的迷雾里，动作几乎成了机械，几乎已经不会听、不会觉。可这垂死的孩子眼里的光，把她带回了眼前可怕的现实。
「啊，哈里！哈里！哈里！」贝蒂只能这样喃喃。
「我要去了，贝蒂。我想——请你替我做一个小小的祷告——再同我道个别，」他喘着说。
贝蒂跪在长凳旁，努力祷告。
「我不愿意跑，贝蒂，可我等啊等啊，没人来，印第安人要进来了。他们会发现外头印第安人的尸首堆成堆。我是一直为你打的，贝蒂，每一回瞄准，我都在想着你。」
孩子絮絮地说着，声音越来越弱，终于没了。那紧攥着贝蒂的手松开了。眼睛闭上了。贝蒂当他死了，可不，他还有气。忽然他睁开眼。痛苦的影子没了，代之闪着一片美丽的光辉。
「贝蒂，我心里一直有你——我要死了——死得快活，因为我为你打过仗——有个声音告诉我——你会——得救。别了。」一丝笑改变了他整张脸，灰眼睛静静地望着她的眼睛。随后他的头向后仰去。一声轻叹，他勇敢的灵魂飞走了。
休·班纳特看了儿子苍白的脸一眼，便跟着赛拉斯和克拉克跑下楼梯。三人从博格斯上尉的小屋后头转出来——那屋子紧挨着棱堡，正挡住他们望南墙的视线——离韦策尔两百英尺。他们听见一根木头撞在栅栏上的沉闷响声；跟着是六英寸厚的栎木板开裂劈碎的声音。又是接连两下狠砸，一块木板的下半截向里倒下去，破口已经容得下一个印第安人。众人朝韦策尔冲过去帮忙，他正举着斧子立在破口旁。就在这一刻，一声枪响。班纳特一个趔趄，栽倒了。一个印第安人从栅栏的洞里开的枪。赛拉斯和阿尔弗雷德斜里朝栅栏闪开，避开了直线。离韦策尔不到二十码时，他们看见一个黑脸膛、精壮的野人从窄缝里挤过来。

===Original===

He had not straightened up before the axe, wielded by the giant hunter, descended on his head, cracking his skull as if it were an eggshell. The savage sank to the earth without even a moan. Another savage, naked and powerful, slipped in. He had to stoop to get through. He raised himself, and seeing Wetzel, he tried to dodge the lightning sweep of the axe. It missed his head, at which it had been aimed, but struck just over the shoulders, and buried itself in flesh and bone. The Indian uttered an agonizing yell which ended in a choking, gurgling sound as the blood spurted from his throat. Wetzel pulled the weapon from the body of his victim, and with the same motion he swung it around. This time the blunt end met the next Indian’s head with a thud like that made by the butcher when he strikes the bullock to the ground. The Indian’s rifle dropped, his tomahawk flew into the air, while his body rolled down the little embankment into the spring. Another and another Indian met the same fate. Then two Indians endeavored to get through the aperture. The awful axe swung by those steel arms dispatched both of them in the twinkling of an eye. Their bodies stuck in the hole.
Silas and Alfred stood riveted to the spot. Just then Wetzel in all his horrible glory was a sight to freeze the marrow of any man. He had cast aside his hunting shirt in that run to the fence and was now stripped to the waist. He was covered with blood. The muscles of his broad back and his brawny arms swelled and rippled under the brown skin. At every swing of the gory axe he let out a yell the like of which had never before been heard by the white men. It was the hunter’s mad yell of revenge. In his thirst for vengeance he had forgotten that he was defending the Fort with its women and its children; he was fighting because he loved to kill.
Silas Zane heard the increasing clamor outside and knew that hundreds of Indians were being drawn to the spot. Something must be done at once. He looked around and his eyes fell on a pile of white oak logs that had been hauled inside the Fort. They had been placed there by Col. Zane, with wise forethought. Silas grabbed Clarke and pulled him toward the pile of logs, at the same time communicating his plan. Together they carried a log to the fence and dropped it in front of the hole.

===Chinese===

他还没直起身，那巨人猎人抡起的斧子已经落到他头上，把他的颅骨劈开，像敲碎一个蛋壳。野人一声没吭就瘫在地上。又一个野人挤进来，赤裸着，精强力壮，进洞得弯着腰。他直起身，一见韦策尔，就想躲过斧子闪电般的一抡。斧子没劈着他脑袋——原是照着脑袋去的——却落在肩上，深深陷进肉里骨里。那印第安人发出一声惨叫，叫声变成咯咯的哽咽，血从喉咙里喷出来。韦策尔把斧子从受难者身上拔出，顺势一抡。这一回是斧背撞上下一个印第安人的头，噢的一声，像屠夫击倒公牛那一击。那印第安人的枪掉了，战斧飞上天，身子滚下小土坡，栽进泉水里。一个又一个印第安人遭到同样的下场。随后两个印第安人想一齐挤进破口。那把可怖的斧子，抡在那双铁臂里，一眨眼把两个都打发掉了。两具尸体卡在洞口。
赛拉斯和阿尔弗雷德钉在了原地。这时的韦策尔，带着他全部可怕的荣光，那模样能把任何人的骨髓冻住。奔向栅栏那一趟他脱了猎衣，此刻光着上身，浑身是血。宽阔的背、粗壮的胳膊上的肌肉，在棕色皮肤下贫起、滚动。那把血斧每抡一下，他吼一声呐喊，白人们从没听见过那样的呐喊。那是猎人复仇的狂吼。复仇的渴欲里，他忘了自己是在守卫堡里的女人孩子；他打仗，只因为他嗜杀。
赛拉斯·赞恩听着外边越来越大的喧声，知道成百的印第安人正朝这里聚。必须立刻想办法。他四下一望，看见一堆白栎木，早先拖进堡里来的——赞恩上校有远见，放在那里。赛拉斯一把拽住克拉克，把他拉向木堆，一面把主意告诉他。两人合力抬了一根木头到栅栏前，横放在破口前。

===Original===

Wetzel immediately stepped on it and took a vicious swing at an Indian who was trying to poke his rifle sideways through the hole. This Indian had discharged his weapon twice. While Wetzel held the Indians at bay, Silas and Clarke piled the logs one upon another, until the hole was closed. This effectually fortified and barricaded the weak place in the stockade fence. The settlers in the bastions were now pouring such a hot fire into the ranks of the savages that they were compelled to retreat out of range.
While Wetzel washed the blood from his arms and his shoulders Silas and Alfred hurried back to where Bennet had fallen. They expected to find him dead, and were overjoyed to see the big settler calmly sitting by the brook binding up a wound in his shoulder.
“It’s nothin’ much. Jest a scratch, but it tumbled me over,” he said. “I was comin’ to help you. That was the wust Injun scrap I ever saw. Why didn’t you keep on lettin’ ’em come in? The red varmints would’a kept on comin’ and Wetzel was good fer the whole tribe. All you’d had to do was to drag the dead Injuns aside and give him elbow room.”
Wetzel joined them at this moment, and they hurried back to the blockhouse. The firing had ceased on the bluff. They met Sullivan at the steps of the Fort. He was evidently coming in search of them.
“Zane, the Indians and the Britishers are getting ready for more determined and persistent effort than any that has yet been made,” said Sullivan.
“How so?” asked Silas.
“They have got hammers from the blacksmith’s shop, and they boarded my boat and found a keg of nails. Now they are making a number of ladders. If they make a rush all at once and place ladders against the fence we’ll have the Fort full of Indians in ten minutes. They can’t stand in the face of a cannon charge. We *must* use the cannon.”
“Clarke, go into Capt. Boggs’s cabin and fetch out two kegs of powder,” said Silas.
The young man turned in the direction of the cabin, while Silas and the others ascended the stairs.
“The firing seems to be all on the south side,” said Silas, “and is not so heavy as it was.”
“Yes, as I said, the Indians on the river front are busy with their new plans,” answered Sullivan.
“Why does not Clarke return?” said Silas, after waiting a few moments at the door of the long room. “We have no time to lose. I want to divide one keg of that powder among the men.”

===Chinese===

韦策尔立刻踏上去，狠狠一斧，劈在一个正想把枪从洞里横着捅进来的印第安人身上——这家伙已经放了两枪。韦策尔堵住印第安人的当口，赛拉斯和克拉克把木头一根压一根地堆上去，直到把洞堵死。栅栏上的薄弱处，就这样牢牢筑死、堵死了。碉楼里的拓荒者又朝野人队伍倾出这样猛烈的火力，他们只好退出射程之外。
韦策尔洗去胳膊和肩膀上的血，赛拉斯和阿尔弗雷德急忙奔回班纳特倒下的地方。他们当他死了，一见这大个子拓荒者安安静静坐在小溪边裹肩上的伤，真是喜出望外。
「不碍事。擦破点皮，就是把我掀了个跟头，」他说。「我正要来帮你们。这回这场印第安人的架，是我见过最凶的。你们干嘛不叫他们一直进来？这帮红崽子会一个劲往里钻，韦策尔一个人就够对付全族。你们只消把死的拖开，给他腾出抡斧子的地方。」
这时韦策尔也到了，众人急忙回棱堡去。崖上的枪声停了。他们在堡门口遇见沙利文，他分明是来找他们的。
「赞恩，印第安人和英国人正准备再干一场，比先前哪一场都更狠、更缠，」沙利文说。
「怎么见得？」赛拉斯问。
「他们从铁匠铺拿了锤子，又上了我的船，寻出一桶钉子。这会儿正赶制一批梯子。要是他们一齐猛冲，把梯子搭上栅栏，十分钟堡里就全是印第安人了。他们受不住炮轰。我们必须用炮。」
「克拉克，去博格斯上尉屋里，搬两桶火药出来，」赛拉斯说。
年轻人转身朝小屋去了，赛拉斯和其余的人上楼。
「枪声好像全在南边，」赛拉斯说，「也不如先前密了。」
「是，我说了，临河这面的印第安人正忙他们的新打算，」沙利文答。
「克拉克怎么还不回来？」赛拉斯在长屋门口等了一会儿，说。「我们没工夫耽搁。我还想拿一桶火药分给弟兄们。」

===Original===

Clarke appeared at the moment. He was breathing heavily as though he had run up the stairs, or was laboring under a powerful emotion. His face was gray.
“I could not find any powder!” he exclaimed. “I searched every nook and corner in Capt. Boggs’s house. There is no powder there.”
A brief silence ensued. Everyone in the blockhouse heard the young man’s voice. No one moved. They all seemed waiting for someone to speak. Finally Silas Zane burst out:
“Not find it? You surely could not have looked well. Capt. Boggs himself told me there were three kegs of powder in the storeroom. I will go and find it myself.”
Alfred did not answer, but sat down on a bench with an odd numb feeling round his heart. He knew what was coming. He had been in the Captain’s house and had seen those kegs of powder. He knew exactly where they had been. Now they were not on the accustomed shelf, nor at any other place in the storeroom. While he sat there waiting for the awful truth to dawn on the garrison, his eyes roved from one end of the room to the other. At last they found what they were seeking. A young woman knelt before a charcoal fire which she was blowing with a bellows. It was Betty. Her face was pale and weary, her hair disheveled, her shapely arms blackened with charcoal, but notwithstanding she looked calm, resolute, self-contained. Lydia was kneeling by her side holding a bullet mold on a block of wood. Betty lifted the ladle from the red coals and poured the hot metal with a steady hand and an admirable precision. Too much or too little lead would make an imperfect ball. The little missile had to be just so for those soft metal, smoothbore rifles. Then Lydia dipped the mold in a bucket of water, removed it and knocked it on the floor. A small, shiny lead bullet rolled out. She rubbed it with a greasy rag and then dropped it in a jar. For nearly forty hours, without sleep or rest, almost without food, those brave girls had been at their post.
Silas Zane came running into the room. His face was ghastly, even his lips were white and drawn.
“Sullivan, in God’s name, what can we do? The powder is gone!” he cried in a strident voice.
“Gone?” repeated several voices.
“Gone?” echoed Sullivan. “Where?”
“God knows. I found where the kegs stood a few days ago. There were marks in the dust. They have been moved.”
“Perhaps Boggs put them here somewhere,” said Sullivan.

===Chinese===

克拉克这会儿出现了。他喘得厉害，像是一口气跑上楼的，又像压着什么激烈的情绪。他脸色发灰。
「我找不到火药！」他喊道。「博格斯上尉屋里，犄角旮旯我都翻遍了。没有火药。」
一阵短促的沉默。棱堡里人人都听见了年轻人的话。谁也没动。人人像在等谁开口。末了赛拉斯·赞恩炸了：
「找不到？你准是没有仔细找。博格斯上尉亲口跟我说，储藏室里有三桶火药。我自己去找。」
阿尔弗雷德没答话，在长凳上坐下，心口周围泛起一种奇怪的麻木。他知道要来了。他到过上尉的屋里，见过那几桶火药，知道它们原本放在哪儿。如今它们不在惯常的架子上，储藏室里任何地方也都没有。他坐在那里，等着这可怕的事实砸到守军头上的工夫，眼睛把屋子从这头扫到那头。终于找到了要找的。一个年轻的姑娘跪在炭火前，正拿风箱吹火。是贝蒂。她脸色苍白疲惫，头发散乱，好看的双臂叫炭火熏黑了，可她看上去镇定、决绝、沉静。莉迪亚跪在她旁边，在一块木头上扶着弹模。贝蒂从红炭上端起小勺，手稳稳的，把滚烫的铅水倒进模里，那份准头真叫人叹服。铅多了少了，弹丸都不合用。这些滑膛枪用的软铅弹，就得这么讲究。莉迪亚把模子往水桶里一蘸，取出来，往地板上一磕，一颗亮晶晶的小铅弹滚出来。她拿油布一擦，丢进罐里。将近四十个钟头，不眠不歇，几乎没吃没喝，这两个勇敢的姑娘一直守在岗位上。
赛拉斯·赞恩跑进屋来。他脸色像鬼，连嘴唇都白了、歪了。
「沙利文，看在上帝份上，我们可怎么办？火药没了！」他声音尖利地喊。
「没了？」好几个声音一齐说。
「没了？」沙利文应道。「哪儿去了？」
「天知道。我找见了几天前放桶的地方，尘土上有印子。桶叫人挪走了。」
「兴许博格斯把它们挪到这儿哪儿了，」沙利文说。

===Original===

“We will look.”
“No use. No use. We were always careful to keep the powder out of here on account of fire. The kegs are gone, gone.”
“Miller stole them,” said Wetzel in his calm voice.
“What difference does that make now?” burst out Silas, turning passionately on the hunter, whose quiet voice in that moment seemed so unfeeling. “They’re gone!”
In the silence which ensued after these words the men looked at each other with slowly whitening faces. There was no need of words. Their eyes told one another what was coming. The fate which had overtaken so many border forts was to be theirs. They were lost! And every man thought not of himself, cared not for himself, but for those innocent children, those brave young girls and heroic women.
A man can die. He is glorious when he calmly accepts death; but when he fights like a tiger, when he stands at bay his back to the wall, a broken weapon in his hand, bloody, defiant, game to the end, then he is sublime. Then he wrings respect from the souls of even his bitterest foes. Then he is avenged even in his death.
But what can women do in times of war? They help, they cheer, they inspire, and if their cause is lost they must accept death or worse. Few women have the courage for self-destruction. “To the victor belong the spoils,” and women have ever been the spoils of war.
No wonder Silas Zane and his men weakened in that moment. With only a few charges for their rifles and none for the cannon how could they hope to hold out against the savages? Alone they could have drawn their tomahawks and have made a dash through the lines of Indians, but with the women and the children that was impossible.
“Wetzel, what can we do? For God’s sake, advise us!” said Silas hoarsely. “We cannot hold the Fort without powder. We cannot leave the women here. We had better tomahawk every woman in the blockhouse than let her fall into the hands of Girty.”
“Send someone fer powder,” answered Wetzel.
“Do you think it possible,” said Silas quickly, a ray of hope lighting up his haggard features. “There’s plenty of powder in Eb’s cabin. Whom shall we send? Who will volunteer?”
Three men stepped forward, and others made a movement.
“They’d plug a man full of lead afore he’d get ten foot from the gate,” said Wetzel. “I’d go myself, but it wouldn’t do no good. Send a boy, and one as can run like a streak.”

===Chinese===

「我们再找找。」
「没用的。没用的。我们一向小心，火药从不搁这儿，怕的就是火。桶没了，没了。」
「米勒偷的，」韦策尔声音平静地说。
「这会儿说这个还有什么分别？」赛拉斯勃然转向猎人发作——那平静的声音，此刻听着竟这样无情。「没了就是没了！」
这几句话之后，一片沉默，男人们面面相觑，脸色慢慢发白。用不着言语。眼睛把要来的事彼此告诉了。落到那么多边地堡子头上的命，要落到他们头上了。他们完了！可人人想的都不是自己，顾的也不是自己，想的是那些无辜的孩子，那些勇敢的年轻姑娘和英勇的女人们。
男人死得起。他从容赴死，是壮烈；可当他像老虎一样厮打，背抵着墙，穷途末路，手里一杆断枪，浑身是血，宁死不屈，战到最后一口气——那他是崇高。那时他从最恨他的仇敌的灵魂里，也夺得敬重。那时他虽死，也算报了仇。
可女人们在战争年月能做什么呢？她们帮忙，她们打气，她们鼓舞；大势若去，她们就只有受死，或者受比死更坏的。有勇气自寻了断的女人，寥寥无几。「战利品归胜者」，女人从来就是战争的战利品。
难怪赛拉斯·赞恩和他的人在那一刻垮了一截。枪里的火药只剩几发，炮一发也没有，叫他们怎么指望顶得住野人？单是他们自己，原可以拔出战斧，朝印第安人的防线拼死冲出去；可有这些女人孩子在，那就办不到。
「韦策尔，怎么办？看上帝份上，给我们拿个主意！」赛拉斯哑着嗓子说。「没有火药守不住堡。我们不能把女人撂在这儿。宁可拿战斧把棱堡里的女人一个个劈了，也别叫她们落到格蒂手里。」
「派人去取火药，」韦策尔答。
「你看行得通？」赛拉斯忙说，一线希望照亮他憔悴的脸。「埃布屋里火药有的是。派谁去？谁肯去？」
三个人跨步上前，旁人也有动的。
「人到不了门口十英尺，就叫他们打成筛子，」韦策尔说。「我倒愿意自己去，可我去没用。派个孩子去，挑一个跑起来像道闪电的。」

===Original===

“There are no lads big enough to carry a keg of powder. Harry Bennett might go,” said Silas. “How is he, Bessie?”
“He is dead,” answered Mrs. Zane.
Wetzel made a motion with his hands and turned away. A short, intense silence followed this indication of hopelessness from him. The women understood, for some of them covered their faces, while others sobbed.
“*I will go.*”
It was Betty’s voice, and it rang clear and vibrant throughout the room. The miserable women raised their drooping heads, thrilled by that fresh young voice. The men looked stupefied. Clarke seemed turned to stone. Wetzel came quickly toward her.
“Impossible!” said Sullivan.
Silas Zane shook his head as if the idea were absurd.
“Let me go, brother, let me go?” pleaded Betty as she placed her little hands softly, caressingly on her brother’s bare arm. “I know it is only a forlorn chance, but still it is a chance. Let me take it. I would rather die that way than remain here and wait for death.”
“Silas, it ain’t a bad plan,” broke in Wetzel. “Betty can run like a deer. And bein’ a woman they may let her get to the cabin without shootin’.”
Silas stood with arms folded across his broad chest. As he gazed at his sister great tears coursed down his dark cheeks and splashed on the hands which so tenderly clasped his own. Betty stood before him transformed; all signs of weariness had vanished; her eyes shone with a fateful resolve; her white and eager face was surpassingly beautiful with its light of hope, of prayer, of heroism.
“Let me go, brother. You know I can run, and oh! I will fly today. Every moment is precious. Who knows? Perhaps Capt. Boggs is already near at hand with help. You cannot *spare* a man. Let me go.”
“Betty, Heaven bless and save you, you shall go,” said Silas.
“No! No! Do not let her go!” cried Clarke, throwing himself before them. He was trembling, his eyes were wild, and he had the appearance of a man suddenly gone mad.
“She shall not go,” he cried.
“What authority have you here?” demanded Silas Zane, sternly. “What right have you to speak?”
“None, unless it is that I love her and I will go for her,” answered Alfred desperately.
“Stand back!” cried Wetzel, placing his powerful hand on Clarke’s breast and pushing him backward. “If you love her you don’t want to have her wait here for them red devils,” and he waved his hand toward the river.

===Chinese===

「没有能扛得动一桶火药的大孩子了。哈里·班纳特倒可以，」赛拉斯说。「他怎么样，贝茜？」
「他死了，」赞恩太太答。
韦策尔摆了摆手，转过身去。他这一下绝望的表示之后，是一阵短促而沉重的寂静。女人们懂了，有的摀住脸，有的哭出声来。
「我去。」
是贝蒂的声音，清亮、震颤，响彻全屋。悲苦的女人们抬起垂着的头，这年轻鲜活的声音叫她们一凛。男人们都怔了。克拉克像化成了石头。韦策尔快步朝她走来。
「不行！」沙利文说。
赛拉斯·赞恩直摇头，像是这主意荒唐透顶。
「叫我去，哥哥，叫我去吧，」贝蒂央求着，把两只小手轻轻柔柔地放在哥哥裸露的胳膊上。「我知道这只是个渺茫的机会，可到底是机会。叫我去吧。我宁可那样死，也不愿留在这儿等死。」
「赛拉斯，这不算个坏主意，」韦策尔插进来。「贝蒂跑起来像头鹿。她是个女人，兴许他们能容她跑到木屋，不打她。」
赛拉斯抱着胳膊站在那里，宽阔的胸膛一起一伏。他望着妹妹，大颗的泪珠顺着黢黑的脸颊滚下来，落在温柔攥着他双手的那双手上。贝蒂站在他面前，换了一个人；疲惫的痕迹全没了；她眼睛里闪着决绝的光；那张急切的、苍白的脸，带着希望、祷告和英勇的光，美得无以复加。
「叫我去，哥哥。你知道我能跑，啊！今天我要飞。一刻工夫都是宝贝。谁知道呢？兴许博格斯上尉已经带着救兵在近处了。你一个男人也抽不出。叫我去吧。」
「贝蒂，上天保佑你、救你，你去吧，」赛拉斯说。
「不！不！别叫她去！」克拉克喊道，抢身挡在他们面前。他浑身发抖，眼睛发直，像是一个突然疯了的人。
「不能叫她去，」他喊。
「你在这儿算老几？」赛拉斯·赞恩厉声质问。「你有什么权利说话？」
「没有权利，除非是我爱她，我替她去，」阿尔弗雷德绝望地答。
「退后！」韦策尔喊，把那只大手指在克拉克胸口，把他推回去。「你要是爱她，就不会想叫她留在这儿等那些红魔鬼，」他朝河那边一挥手。

===Original===

“If she gets back she’ll save the Fort. If she fails she’ll at least escape Girty.”
Betty gazed into the hunter’s eyes and then into Alfred’s. She understood both men. One was sending her out to her death because he knew it would be a thousand times more merciful than the fate which awaited her at the hands of the Indians. The other had not the strength to watch her go to her death. He had offered himself rather than see her take such fearful chances.
“I know. If it were possible you would both save me,” said Betty, simply. “Now you can do nothing but pray that God may spare my life long enough to reach the gate. Silas, I am ready.”
Downstairs a little group of white-faced men were standing before the gateway. Silas Zane had withdrawn the iron bar. Sullivan stood ready to swing in the ponderous gate. Wetzel was speaking with a clearness and a rapidity which were wonderful under the circumstances.
“When we let you out you’ll have a clear path. Run, but not very fast. Save your speed. Tell the Colonel to empty a keg of powder in a table cloth. Throw it over your shoulder and start back. Run like you was racin’ with me, and keep on comin’ if you do get hit. Now go!”
The huge gate creaked and swung in. Betty ran out, looking straight before her. She had covered half the distance between the Fort and the Colonel’s house when long taunting yells filled the air.
“Squaw! Waugh! Squaw! Waugh!” yelled the Indians in contempt.
Not a shot did they fire. The yells ran all along the river front, showing that hundreds of Indians had seen the slight figure running up the gentle slope toward the cabin.
Betty obeyed Wetzel’s instructions to the letter. She ran easily and not at all hurriedly, and was as cool as if there had not been an Indian within miles.
Col. Zane had seen the gate open and Betty come forth. When she bounded up the steps he flung open that door and she ran into his arms.
“Betts, for God’s sake! What’s this?” he cried.
“We are out of powder. Empty a keg of powder into a table cloth. Quick! I’ve not a second to lose,” she answered, at the same time slipping off her outer skirt. She wanted nothing to hinder that run for the blockhouse.
Jonathan Zane heard Betty’s first words and disappeared into the magazine room. He came out with a keg in his arms. With one blow of an axe he smashed in the top of the keg.

===Chinese===

「她要是回来，就救了堡子。她要是败了，至少逃开了格蒂。」
贝蒂望望猎人的眼睛，又望望阿尔弗雷德的。她懂得这两个男人。一个送她去赴死，因为他知道，那比等着印第安人给她的下场，仁慈一千倍。另一个没有眼睁睁看她去死的力气。他宁可自己去，也不忍见她冒这样可怕的险。
「我知道。要是办得到，你们俩都肯救我，」贝蒂简单地说。「这会儿，你们只有祷告上帝，叫我多活一口气，跑到大门。赛拉斯，我准备好了。」
楼下，一小群脸白白的男人立在门口。赛拉斯·赞恩抽开铁门闩。沙利文站好，等着推开那扇沉重的门。韦策尔交代着话，又清楚又利落，在那样的关头真是难得。
「放你出去，路是空的。跑，先别使全力。留着劲。叫上校把一桶火药倒在桌布上。搭上肩头就往回跑。就当是同我赛跑，捱了枪也别停。去吧！」
大门吱呀一声荡开。贝蒂跑出去，眼睛直望前方。她跑完堡子到上校宅子那段路的一半，空气里响起了长长的嘲弄的呐喊。
「婆娘！哇！婆娘！哇！」印第安人轻蔑地喊。
他们一枪也没放。呐喊声顺着临河一面传开去，可见几百个印第安人都看见了这个苗条的身影，正顺着缓坡往木屋跑。
贝蒂把韦策尔的嘱咐照办得分毫不差。她跑得从容，一点不显慌，镇定得仿佛方圆几里没有一个印第安人。
赞恩上校看见堡门开了，贝蒂出来。她跃上台阶，他一把甩开门，她扑进他怀里。
「贝茨，老天爷！这是怎么了？」他喊。
「我们没火药了。把一桶火药倒在桌布上。快！我一秒也不能耽搁，」她答着，一面褪下外面的裙子。什么都不能拖累她那趟回棱堡的奔跑。
乔纳森·赞恩听见贝蒂头一句话，就不见了，进了弹药房，抱着一桶火药出来。一斧头，他砸开了桶盖。

===Original===

In a twinkling a long black stream of the precious stuff was piling up in a little hill in the center of the table. Then the corners of the table cloth were caught up, turned and twisted, and the bag of powder was thrown over Betty’s shoulder.
“Brave girl, so help me God, you are going to do it!” cried Col. Zane, throwing open the door. “I know you can. Run as you never ran in all your life.”
Like an arrow sprung from a bow Betty flashed past the Colonel and out on the green. Scarcely ten of the long hundred yards had been covered by her flying feet when a roar of angry shouts and yells warned Betty that the keen-eyed savages saw the bag of powder and now knew they had been deceived by a girl. The cracking of rifles began at a point on the bluff nearest Col. Zane’s house, and extended in a half circle to the eastern end of the clearing. The leaden messengers of Death whistled past Betty. They sped before her and behind her, scattering pebbles in her path, striking up the dust, and ploughing little furrows in the ground. A quarter of the distance covered! Betty had passed the top of the knoll now and she was going down the gentle slope like the wind. None but a fine marksman could have hit that small, flitting figure. The yelling and screeching had become deafening. The reports of the rifles blended in a roar. Yet above it all Betty heard Wetzel’s stentorian yell. It lent wings to her feet. Half the distance covered! A hot, stinging pain shot through Betty’s arm, but she heeded it not. The bullets were raining about her. They sang over her head; hissed close to her ears, and cut the grass in front of her; they pattered like hail on the stockade fence, but still untouched, unharmed, the slender brown figure sped toward the gate. Three-fourths of the distance covered! A tug at the flying hair, and a long, black tress cut off by a bullet, floated away on the breeze. Betty saw the big gate swing; she saw the tall figure of the hunter; she saw her brother. Only a few more yards! On! On! On! A blinding red mist obscured her sight. She lost the opening in the fence, but unheeding she rushed on. Another second and she stumbled; she felt herself grasped by eager arms; she heard the gate slam and the iron bar shoot into place; then she felt and heard no more.
Silas Zane bounded up the stairs with a doubly precious burden in his arms.

===Chinese===

转眼间，一条长长的黑色细流，在桌面上堆起一座小山。随后桌布四角拎起，一拧一扎，这包火药就搭上了贝蒂的肩头。
「勇敢的姑娘，上天保佑，你一定办得到！」赞恩上校喊着，把门敞开。「我知道你行。这辈子怎么跑的，今天都比那跑得更快。」
贝蒂像离弦的箭，从上校身边一闪，上了草坪。长长的百码路，她那双飞奔的脚才踏上十码，一阵怒吼般的呐喊就叫贝蒂知道：眼尖的野人看见那包火药了，明白叫一个姑娘耍了。步枪的爆响，从离上校宅子最近的崖头一点上开始，沿着弧线一路延到空地的东头。铅铸的死亡使者从贝蒂身边呼啸而过。它们赶在她前头，追在她后头，把小石子崩得四散，打得尘土飞溅，在地上犁出一条条小沟。四分之一的路跑完了！贝蒂过了小丘顶，顺着缓坡像风一样往下冲。只有顶好的枪手才打得中那样一个小小的、掠过去的身影。呐喊尖叫震耳欲聋。枪声连成一片轰响。可这一切之上，贝蒂听见了韦策尔洪亮的呐喊。它给她的脚添了翅膀。一半的路跑完了！一阵火辣辣的剧痛穿过贝蒂的胳膊，她顾不上。子弹在她四周落着雨。它们从她头顶上唱过去，贴着耳朵嘶，割断她面前的草；它们冰雹似的打在栅栏上；可那纤细的棕色身影依然没沾一枪、没带一点伤，直朝大门飞去。四分之三的路跑完了！飞跑的头发被一股力拽了一下，一绰长长的黑发叫子弹剪断，随风飘走。贝蒂看见大门荡开；看见猎人高大的身影；看见她的哥哥。只剩几码了！冲！冲！冲！一片发红的雾蒙住了她的眼。她看不见栅栏的口子了，可她不管，只顾往前冲。又一下，她绊倒了；她觉得自己被急切的手臂抓住；她听见大门砰地合上，铁闩哐当插进槽里；随后她什么也听不见，什么也觉不着了。
赛拉斯·赞恩抱着双倍的珍宝，大步奔上楼梯。

===Original===

A mighty cheer greeted his entrance. It aroused Alfred Clarke, who had bowed his head on the bench and had lost all sense of time and place. What were the women sobbing and crying over? To whom belonged that white face? Of course, it was the face of the girl he loved. The face of the girl who had gone to her death. And he writhed in his agony.
Then something wonderful happened. A warm, living flush swept over that pale face. The eyelids fluttered; they opened, and the dark eyes, radiant, beautiful, gazed straight into Alfred’s.
Still Alfred could not believe his eyes. That pale face and the wonderful eyes belonged to the ghost of his sweetheart. They had come back to haunt him. Then he heard a voice.
“O‑h! but that brown place burns!”
Alfred saw a bare and shapely arm. Its beauty was marred by a cruel red welt. He heard that same sweet voice laugh and cry together. Then he came back to life and hope. With one bound he sprang to a porthole.
“God, what a woman!” he said between his teeth, as he thrust the rifle forward.
It was indeed not a time for inaction. The Indians, realizing they had been tricked and had lost a golden opportunity, rushed at the Fort with renewed energy. They attacked from all sides and with the persistent fury of savages long disappointed in their hopes. They were received with a scathing, deadly fire. Bang! roared the cannon, and the detachment of savages dropped their ladders and fled. The little “bull dog” was turned on its swivel and directed at another rush of Indians. Bang! and the bullets, chainlinks, and bits of iron ploughed through the ranks of the enemy. The Indians never lived who could stand in the face of well-aimed cannon-shot. They fell back. The settlers, inspired, carried beyond themselves by the heroism of a girl, fought as they had never fought before. Every shot went to a redskin’s heart. Impelled by the powder for which a brave girl had offered her life, guided by hands and arms of iron, and aimed by eyes as fixed and stern as Fate, every bullet shed the lifeblood of a warrior.
Slowly and sullenly the red men gave way before that fire. Foot by foot they retired. Girty was seen no more. Fire, the Shawnee chief, lay dead in the road almost in the same spot where two days before his brother chief, Red Fox, had bit the dust. The British had long since retreated.

===Chinese===

他一进门，一片响亮的欢呼迎着他。这欢呼唤醒了阿尔弗雷德·克拉克——他伏在长凳上，早已失去了时间和地点的知觉。女人们哭什么？那张苍白的脸是谁的？当然，是他爱的姑娘的脸，那去赴死的姑娘的脸。他痛苦地扭动着。
这时，奇妙的事发生了。一股温热的、活生生的红润，漫过那张苍白的脸。眼皮颤了；睁开了；那双黑眼睛，亮得耀眼，美得惊人，直直望进阿尔弗雷德的眼睛。
阿尔弗雷德还是不敢信自己的眼睛。那苍白的脸、那双奇妙的眼睛，属于他心上人的魂灵，是回来缠他的。随后他听见一个声音。
「哎——哟！那块擦破的地方好疼！」
阿尔弗雷德看见一条裸露的、好看的手臂，那美给一道狠毒的红肿破坏了。他听见那甜甜的嗓音又笑又哭。于是他活了过来，有了指望。他一跃而起，扑到一个枪眼前。
「天哪，这样的女人！」他咬着牙说，一面把枪筒递出去。
这实在不是闲着的时辰。印第安人明白自己受了捉弄、错过了一个黄金机会，鼓起新的狠劲朝堡子猛扑。他们从四面进攻，带着野人宿愿落空之后那种不休的狂怒。迎接他们的是痛烈的、致命的火力。轰！大炮怒吼，那一小股野人丢下梯子逃了。小「猛犬」调过转轴，对准又一股冲锋的印第安人。轰！弹丸、链环、碎铁犁开敌阵。从来还没有印第安人能站得住瞄得准的炮火。他们退了。拓荒者们受一个姑娘的英勇感召，忘掉了自己，打得从没有这样狠过。每一枪都打进一个红人的心。有勇敢的姑娘拿命换来的火药催送，有铁打的手臂引导，有如命运一般沉着冷酷的眼睛瞄准，每一颗子弹都放走一个武士的血。
红人在这样的火力前，一步一步、恨恨地让开。一尺一尺地退。格蒂再没露面。肖尼酋长「火」死在大路上，几乎正是两天前他的兄弟酋长红狐倒下的地方。英国人早就退走了。

===Original===

When night came the exhausted and almost famished besiegers sought rest and food.
The moon came out clear and beautiful, as if ashamed of her traitor’s part of the night before, and brightened up the valley, bathing the Fort, the river, and the forest in her silver light.
Shortly after daybreak the next morning the Indians, despairing of success, held a powwow. While they were grouped in plain view of the garrison, and probably conferring over the question of raising the siege, the long, peculiar whoop of an Indian spy, who had been sent out to watch for the approach of a relief party, rang out. This seemed a signal for retreat. Scarcely had the shrill cry ceased to echo in the hills when the Indians and the British, abandoning their dead, moved rapidly across the river.
After a short interval a mounted force was seen galloping up the creek road. It proved to be Capt. Boggs, Swearengen, and Williamson with seventy men. Great was the rejoicing! Capt. Boggs had expected to find only the ashes of the Fort. And the gallant little garrison, although saddened by the loss of half its original number, rejoiced that it had repulsed the united forces of braves and British.

===Chinese===

黑夜来临，精疲力竭、几乎断粮的围攻者寻他们的吃住去了。
月亮出来了，明净可爱，像是羞惭自己前一夜充当的奸细角色，照亮河谷，把堡子、河流和森林都浸在银辉里。
第二天清早，天一亮不久，印第安人断了得胜的念头，聚在一起商量。他们就在守军眼皮底下围成一团，多半正在计议撤围，忽然响起一声印第安探子的长而独特的呐喊——那是派出去守望援兵的。这一声像是撤退的号令。尖啸的余音还没在山里散尽，印第安人和英国人已经丢下死者，飞速渡河而去。
不大的工夫，一支骑队顺着溪边大路疾驰而来。原来是博格斯上尉、斯韦林根和威廉森，带着七十个人。欢声雷动！博格斯上尉原以为只能找到堡子的灰烬。这支英勇的小小的守军，虽然痛惜折了原来一半的人，却也欢欣——他们打退了野人和英国人的联军。

## XV / 十五
===Original===

Peace and quiet reigned ones more at Ft. Henry. Before the glorious autumn days had waned, the settlers had repaired the damage done to their cabins, and many of them were now occupied with the fall plowing. Never had the Fort experienced such busy days. Many new faces were seen in the little meeting house. Pioneers from Virginia, from Ft. Pitt, and eastward had learned that Fort Henry had repulsed the biggest force of Indians and soldiers that Governor Hamilton and his minions could muster. Settlers from all points along the river were flocking to Col. Zane’s settlement. New cabins dotted the hillside; cabins and barns in all stages of construction could be seen. The sounds of hammers, the ringing stroke of the axe, and the crashing down of mighty pines or poplars were heard all day long.
Col. Zane sat oftener and longer than ever before in his favorite seat on his doorstep. On this evening he had just returned from a hard day in the fields, and sat down to rest a moment before going to supper. A few days previous Isaac Zane and Myeerah had come to the settlement. Myeerah brought a treaty of peace signed by Tarhe and the other Wyandot chieftains. The once implacable Huron was now ready to be friendly with the white people. Col. Zane and his brothers signed the treaty, and Betty, by dint of much persuasion, prevailed on Wetzel to bury the hatchet with the Hurons. So Myeerah’s love, like the love of many other women, accomplished more than years of war and bloodshed.
The genial and happy smile never left Col. Zane’s face, and as he saw the well-laden rafts coming down the river, and the air of liveliness and animation about the growing settlement, his smile broadened into one of pride and satisfaction. The prophecy that he had made twelve years before was fulfilled. His dream was realized. The wild, beautiful spot where he had once built a bark shack and camped half a year without seeing a white man was now the scene of a bustling settlement; and he believed he would live to see that settlement grow into a prosperous city. He did not think of the thousands of acres which would one day make him a wealthy man. He was a pioneer at heart; he had opened up that rich new country; he had conquered all obstacles, and that was enough to make him content.
“Papa, when shall I be big enough to fight bars and bufflers and Injuns?”

===Chinese===

和平安宁重又降临亨利堡。辉煌的秋日尚未消尽，拓荒者已修好了各家的木屋，许多人又忙起秋耕来。堡子从来没有这样忙过。小礼拜堂里出现许多新面孔。弗吉尼亚、皮特堡和东边来的拓荒者听说亨利堡打退了汉密尔顿总督和他的爪牙所能纠集的最大一支印第安人和士兵的联军，沿河各处的移民纷纷涌向赞恩上校的移民地。山坡上新木屋星星点点；到处是建造到一半的木屋和谷仓。锤子声、斧子的清脆砍声、大松大杨轰然倒地的声音，整天不断。
赞恩上校比以往更久、更勤地坐在他喜欢的门口台阶上。这天傍晚，他在地里劳碌了一天刚回来，先坐一坐，歇口气再吃饭。几天前，艾萨克·赞恩和米伊拉到了移民地。米伊拉带来一份和约，塔赫和其余怀安多特酋长都签了字。那个从前不共戴天的休伦人，如今愿意同白人交好了。赞恩上校和几个弟弟签了和约；贝蒂又好说歹说，劝得韦策尔同休伦人埋了战斧。米伊拉的爱，像许多别的女人的爱一样，办成了多年战争和流血办不成的事。
赞恩上校脸上那副乐呵呵的笑从没退过；看着满载的木排顺河而下，看着日益兴旺的移民地上一派生机，他的笑意更宽了，成了得意和满足。他十二年前说的预言应验了。他的梦实现了。这片他曾搭一座树皮棚、住了半年没见一个白人的荒野佳境，如今是一片兴旺的移民地；他相信，自己能活着看到它长成一座繁华的城市。他没去想那几千英亩地有一天会使他成为富人。他心里是个拓荒者；他开辟了这片富饶的新国土；他战胜了一切艰难，这就够他知足了。
「爸爸，我什么时候才能长得够大，去打熊、打野牛、打印第安人？」

===Original===

asked Noah, stopping in his play and straddling his father’s knee.
“My boy, did you not have Indians enough a short time ago?”
“But, papa, I did not get to see any. I heard the shooting and yelling. Sammy was afraid, but I wasn’t. I wanted to look out of the little holes, but they locked us up in the dark room.”
“If that boy ever grows up to be like Jonathan or Wetzel it will be the death of me,” said the Colonel’s wife, who had heard the lad’s chatter.
“Don’t worry, Bessie. When Noah grows to be a man the Indians will be gone.”
Col. Zane heard the galloping of a horse and looking up saw Clarke coming down the road on his black thoroughbred. The Colonel rose and walked out to the hitching block, where Clarke had reined in his fiery steed.
“Ah, Alfred. Been out for a ride?”
“Yes, I have been giving Roger a little exercise.”
“That’s a magnificent animal. I never get tired watching him move. He’s the best bit of horseflesh on the river. By the way, we have not seen much of you since the siege. Of course you have been busy. Getting ready to put on the harness, eh? Well, that’s what we want the young men to do. Come over and see us.”
“I have been trying to come. You know how it is with me﻿—about Betty, I mean. Col. Zane, I﻿—I love her. That’s all.”
“Yes, I know, Alfred, and I don’t wonder at your fears. But I have always liked you, and now I guess it’s about time for me to put a spoke in your wheel of fortune. If Betty cares for you﻿—and I have a sneaking idea she does﻿—I will give her to you.”
“I have nothing. I gave up everything when I left home.”
“My lad, never mind about that,” said the Colonel, laying his hand on Clarke’s knee. “We don’t need riches. I have so often said that we need nothing out here on the border but honest hearts and strong, willing hands. These you have. That is enough for me and for my people, and as for land, why, I have enough for an army of young men. I got my land cheap. That whole island there I bought from Cornplanter. You can have that island or any tract of land along the river. Some day I shall put you at the head of my men. It will take you years to cut that road through to Maysville. Oh, I have plenty of work for you.”
“Col. Zane, I cannot thank you,” answered Alfred, with emotion. “I shall try to merit your friendship and esteem. Will you please tell your sister I shall come over in the morning and beg to see her alone.”

===Chinese===

诺亚停下游耍，跨到父亲膝上，问。
「孩子，前几天印第安人你还没受够？」
「可是爸爸，我压根儿没看见。我听见打枪喊叫。萨米害怕，我不怕。我想从小洞往外瞧，可他们把我们锁在黑屋子里。」
「这孩子要是长大了学乔纳森或韦策尔的样，会叫我愁死，」上校太太听见了孩子的话，说。
「别愁，贝茜。等诺亚长大成人，印第安人早没影了。」
赞恩上校听见一阵马蹄声，抬头看见克拉克骑着他的黑色纯种马沿大路过来。上校起身走到拴马石旁，克拉克已在那儿勒住他那匹烈马。
「啊，阿尔弗雷德。出去遛马了？」
「是，给罗杰活动活动筋骨。」
「好一匹骏马。看它走动，我总看不腻。沿河上下数它第一。对了，围城以后很少见你了。当然，你忙。就要上套拉车了，嗯？好，这正是我们要年轻人干的。常来坐。」
「我一直想来。您知道我的情形——我是说，为着贝蒂。赞恩上校，我——我爱她。就是这一句。」
「是，我知道，阿尔弗雷德，你怕，我不奇怪。可我一向喜欢你，如今我想，该是我在你的运道轮子上插一手的时辰了。只要贝蒂心里有你——我偷偷瞧着是有——我就把她许给你。」
「我一无所有。离家时把一切都舍了。」
「孩子，那个不用愁，」上校说着，把手放在克拉克膝上。「我们不需要钱财。我常说，边地上我们什么都不缺，只缺诚实的心、结实肯干的手。这些你都有。这对我、对我家里人就够了。至于地——嘨，我的地，够一支军队的年轻人分的。我的地来得便宜。那边那整座岛，就是从康普兰特手里买的。那座岛给你也行，沿河哪一片给你也行。往后我还要叫你领我的人。修那条直通梅斯维尔的路，就得你忙好几年。哦，有的是活儿给你干。」
「赞恩上校，我不知道怎么谢您，」阿尔弗雷德动情地说。「我一定不辜负您的情分和看重。求您告诉令妹，明早我过去，求她单独见我。」

===Original===

“That I will, Alfred. Goodnight.”
Col. Zane strode across his threshold with a happy smile on his face. He loved to joke and tease, and never lost an opportunity.
“Things seem to be working out all right. Now for some fun with Her Highness,” he said to himself.
As the Colonel surveyed the pleasant home scene he felt he had nothing more to wish for. The youngsters were playing with a shaggy little pup which had already taken Tige’s place in their fickle affections. His wife was crooning a lullaby as she gently rocked the cradle to and fro. A wonderful mite of humanity peacefully slumbered in that old cradle. Annie was beginning to set the table for the evening meal. Isaac lay with a contented smile on his face, fast asleep on the couch, where, only a short time before, he had been laid bleeding and almost dead. Betty was reading to Myeerah, whose eyes were rapturously bright as she leaned her head against her sister and listened to the low voice.
“Well, Betty, what do you think?” said Col. Zane, stopping before the girls.
“What do I think?” retorted Betty. “Why, I think you are very rude to interrupt me. I am reading to Myeerah her first novel.”
“I have a very important message for you.”
“For me? What! From whom?”
“Guess.”
Betty ran through a list of most of her acquaintances, but after each name her brother shook his head.
“Oh, well, I don’t care,” she finally said. The color in her cheeks had heightened noticeably.
“Very well. If you do not care, I will say nothing more,” said Col. Zane.
At this juncture Annie called them to supper. Later, when Col. Zane sat on the doorstep smoking, Betty came and sat beside him with her head resting against his shoulder. The Colonel smoked on in silence. Presently the dusky head moved restlessly.
“Eb, tell me the message,” whispered Betty.
“Message? What message?” asked Col. Zone. “What are you talking about?”
“Do not tease﻿—not now. Tell me.” There was an undercurrent of wistfulness in Betty’s voice which touched the kindhearted brother.
“Well, today a certain young man asked me if he could relieve me of the responsibility of looking after a certain young lady.”
“Oh﻿—”
“Wait a moment. I told him I would be delighted.”
“Eb, that was unkind.”
“Then he asked me to tell her he was coming over tomorrow morning to fix it up with her.”
“Oh, horrible!” cried Betty. “Were those the words he used?”

===Chinese===

「一定带到，阿尔弗雷德。晚安。」
赞恩上校跨进家门，脸上挂着得意的笑。他就爱打趣逗乐，逢机会不肯放过。
「事情看着都顺遂。这会儿去同『殿下』逗逗乐子，」他自言自语。
上校打量这满屋安乐光景，觉着再没什么可求的了。孩子们正逗一只毛茸茸的小狗玩——在他们易变的爱心里，它已经顶了蒂格的缺。妻子轻轻摇着摇篮，哼着眠歌。摇篮里那个小小的人儿安安稳稳睡着。安妮开始摆晚饭的碗筷。艾萨克躺在长榻上，睡得又香又甜，嘴角挂着满足的笑——不久以前，他还浑身是血、只剩一口气地躺在那儿。贝蒂正念书给米伊拉听，米伊拉头靠着妹妹，听得两眼发亮。
「喂，贝蒂，你猜怎么着？」赞恩上校在姑娘们跟前站住，说。
「猜怎么着？」贝蒂反问。「我看你是失礼，打搅人家。我在给米伊拉念她这辈子头一部小说呢。」
「我有一件要紧的口信给你。」
「给我？什么！谁捎的？」
「猜。」
贝蒂把熟人几乎数了个遍，每个名字她哥哥都摇头。
「哦，好啊，我才不在乎呢，」她末了说。腮上的红明显深了。
「很好。你不在乎，我就不多嘴了，」赞恩上校说。
正说着，安妮请他们吃晚饭。后来，赞恩上校坐在门口台阶上抽烟，贝蒂走出来挨他坐下，头靠在他肩上。上校闷头抽烟。过了一会儿，那颗乌黑的头不安地动了动。
「埃布，告诉我那个口信，」贝蒂悄声说。
「口信？什么口信？」赞恩上校问。「你说什么呢？」
「别逗了——这会儿别逗。告诉我。」贝蒂话音里那一点渴望，打动了好心的哥哥。
「好吧。今天有个小伙子问我，能不能替我分劳，把照看一位小姐的担子接过去。」
「哦——」
「等等。我告诉他，我求之不得。」
「埃布，你这就刻薄了。」
「随后他求我告诉那位小姐，明早他过来，同她把这事定妥。」
「哦，好吓人！」贝蒂喊。「他真是这么说的？」

===Original===

“Betts, to tell the honest truth, he did not say much of anything. He just said: ‘I love her,’ and his eyes blazed.”
Betty uttered a half articulate cry and ran to her room. Her heart was throbbing. What could she do? She felt that if she looked once into her lover’s eyes she would have no strength. How dared she allow herself to be so weak! Yet she knew this was the end. She could deceive him no longer. For she felt a stir in her heart, stronger than all, beyond all resistance, an exquisite agony, the sweet, blind, tumultuous exultation of the woman who loves and is loved.
---
“Bess, what do you think?” said Col. Zane, going into the kitchen next morning, after he had returned from the pasture. “Clarke just came over and asked for Betty. I called her. She came down looking as sweet and cool as one of the lilies out by the spring. She said: ‘Why, Mr. Clarke, you are almost a stranger. I am pleased to see you. Indeed, we are all very glad to know you have recovered from your severe burns.’ She went on talking like that for all the world like a girl who didn’t care a snap for him. And she knows as well as I do. Not only that, she has been actually breaking her heart over him all these months. How did she do it? Oh, you women beat me all hollow!”
“Would you expect Betty to fall into his arms?” asked the Colonel’s worthy spouse, indignantly.
“Not exactly. But she was too cool, too friendly. Poor Alfred looked as if he hadn’t slept. He was nervous and scared to death. When Betty ran upstairs I put a bug in Alfred’s ear. He’ll be all right now, if he follows my advice.”
“Humph! What did Colonel Ebenezer Zane tell him?” asked Bessie, in disgust.
“Oh, not much. I simply told him not to lose his nerve; that a woman never meant ‘no’; that she often says it only to be made say ‘yes.’ And I ended up with telling him if she got a little skittish, as thoroughbreds do sometimes, to try a strong arm. That was my way.”
“Col. Zane, if my memory does not fail me, you were as humble and beseeching as the proudest girl could desire.”
“I beseeching? Never!”
“I hope Alfred’s wooing may go well. I like him very much. But I’m afraid. Betty has such a spirit that it is quite likely she will refuse him for no other reason than that he built his cabin before he asked her.”
“Nonsense. He asked her long ago. Never fear, Bess, my sister will come back as meek as a lamb.”

===Chinese===

「贝茨，说老实话，他没说出什么来。他只说：『我爱她。』眼睛直冒火。」
贝蒂啊了半声，跑回自己屋里去了。她的心怦怦直跳。她可怎么办？她知道，只要朝爱人的眼睛看上一眼，她就会一丝力气也没有。她怎么敢由着自己这样软弱！可她知道，这是尽头了。她再骗不了他。因为她觉出心里有一阵骚动，比一切都强，无可抗拒——一种甜蜜的煎熬，一个爱着也被爱着的女人那又甜又盲目、又乱又欢喜的激动。
---
「贝丝，你猜怎么着？」第二天早上，赞恩上校从牧场回来，走进厨房说。「克拉克刚过来求见贝蒂。我叫了她。她下了楼，又甜又清爽，活像泉边的一朵百合。她说：『哎呀，克拉克先生，您都快成生人了。见到您真高兴。真的，您那场重伤好了，我们都替您欢喜。』她就这么一路说下去，活脱脱一个拿他半个子儿也不放在心上的姑娘。可她心里跟我一样明白。岂止明白，这几个月她实打实为他心碎呢。她这是怎么办到的？嘨，你们女人，我算是服到家了！」
「怎么，你要贝蒂一头栽进他怀里？」上校那位贤德的太太愤然问。
「那倒不必。可她也太淡太客气了。可怜的阿尔弗雷德，一脸没睡好的样儿，紧张得要死。贝蒂上楼以后，我给阿尔弗雷德递了一句悄悄话。他这会儿行了，只要他照我的话办。」
「哼！埃比尼泽·赞恩上校给他说了什么？」贝茜嗤之以鼻。
「哦，没什么。我只叫他别泄气；说女人嘴里从来没有『不』字；她说『不』，往往只是等人逼她说『是』。末了又告诉他，她要是撒起性子来——纯种马有时就这样——就使出男人手段来。我就是这么过来的。」
「赞恩上校，要是我没记错，当年您那份低声下气、苦苦哀求，最骄傲的姑娘也挑不出毛病。」
「我哀求？从来没有！」
「我盼着阿尔弗雷德求婚顺利。我很喜欢他。可是我怕。贝蒂那股子烈性，很可能就为他『先盖了屋才开口』这一个由头，回了他。」
「胡说。他早就开过口了。别愁，贝丝，我妹妹回来，会乖得像只羊羔。」

===Original===

Meanwhile Betty and Alfred were strolling down the familiar path toward the river. The October air was fresh with a suspicion of frost. The clear notes of a hunter’s horn came floating down from the hills. A flock of wild geese had alighted on the marshy ground at the end of the island where they kept up a continual honk! honk! The brown hills, the red forest, and the yellow fields were now at the height of their autumnal beauty. Soon the November north wind would thrash the trees bare, and bow the proud heads of the daisies and the goldenrod; but just now they flashed in the sun, and swayed back and forth in all their glory.
“I see you limp. Are you not entirely well?” Betty was saying.
“Oh, I am getting along famously, thank you,” said Alfred. “This one foot was quite severely burned and is still tender.”
“You have had your share of injuries. I heard my brother say you had been wounded three times within a year.”
“Four times.”
“Jonathan told of the axe wound; then the wound Miller gave you, and finally the burns. These make three, do they not?”
“Yes, but you see, all three could not be compared to the one you forgot to mention.”
“Let us hurry past here,” said Betty, hastening to change the subject. “This is where you had the dreadful fight with Miller.”
“As Miller did go to meet Girty, and as he did not return to the Fort with the renegade, we must believe he is dead. Of course, we do not know this to be actually a fact. But something makes me think so. Jonathan and Wetzel have not said anything; I can’t get any satisfaction on that score from either; but I am sure neither of them would rest until Miller was dead.”
“I think you are right. But we may never know. All I can tell you is that Wetzel and Jack trailed Miller to the river, and then they both came back. I was the last to see Lewis that night before he left on Miller’s trail. It isn’t likely I shall forget what Lewis said and how he looked. Miller was a wicked man; yes, a traitor.”
“He was a bad man, and he nearly succeeded in every one of his plans. I have not the slightest doubt that had he refrained from taking part in the shooting match he would have succeeded in abducting you, in killing me, and in leading Girty here long before he was expected.”
“There are many things that may never be explained, but one thing Miller did always mystifies us. How did he succeed in binding Tige?”

===Chinese===

这会儿，贝蒂和阿尔弗雷德正顺着熟路往河边走。十月的空气里带着一丝霜意，清新得很。山上飘下猎人号角清亮的调子。一群大雁落在岛尾的沼地上，不住地嘎嘎！嘎嘎！褐色的山，红色的林，黄色的田野，正是秋色最盛的时候。不要多久，十一月的北风就会把树打得精光，压下雏菊和秋麒麟那骄傲的头；可此刻它们还在太阳里闪光，摇曳着，一身的辉煌。
「我看你走路还瘸。没好利索？」贝蒂正说着。
「哦，好得甭提了，谢谢，」阿尔弗雷德说。「这只脚烧得不轻，还嫩着。」
「你受的伤也够数了。听哥哥说，你一年里挂了三回彩。」
「四回。」
「乔纳森说了斧子那回，米勒给你的那回，末了是烧的。这是三回，不是？」
「是。可你瞧，这三回加在一起，也比不上你忘了提的那一回。」
「这儿我们快些走过去，」贝蒂说，急忙岔开话头。「这就是你同米勒恶斗的地方。」
「米勒既然真去会了格蒂，又没同那叛徒回堡里来，我们只能当他死了。当然，这是不是实情，我们无从知道。可我总这么觉得。乔纳森和韦策尔一个字不提；这事我问谁也问不出个所以然；可我拿得准，米勒不死，他们俩谁也安不下心。」
「我想你说得对。可我们也许永远不知道了。我只有一句：韦策尔和杰克把米勒的踪迹追到了河边，随后两个人就回来了。卢动身去追米勒那夜，我是最后一个见到他的。路易斯说了什么、那副模样，我这辈子忘不了。米勒是个恶人；是的，一个叛徒。」
「他是个坏人，可他哪一桩毒计都差点得手。我一点也不怀疑，那天他若不去凑打靶的热闹，他准能把你抢走，把我杀掉，还能领着格蒂提前赶到这儿。」
「有许多事只怕永远说不清了，可米勒干的一件事，我们到今天还纳闷。他是怎么把蒂格捆住的？」

===Original===

“To my way of thinking that was not so difficult as climbing into my room and almost killing me, or stealing the powder from Capt. Boggs’s room.”
“The last, at least, gave me a chance to help,” said Betty, with a touch of her odd roguishness.
“That was the grandest thing a woman ever did,” said Alfred, in a low tone.
“Oh, no, I only ran fast.”
“I would have given the world to have seen you, but I was lying on the bench wishing I were dead. I did not have strength to look out of a porthole. Oh! that horrible time! I can never forget it. I lie awake at night and hear the yelling and shooting. Then I dream of running over the burning roofs and it all comes back so vividly I can almost feel the flames and smell the burnt wood. Then I wake up and think of that awful moment when you were carried into the blockhouse white, and, as I thought, dead.”
“But I wasn’t. And I think it best for us to forget that horrible siege. It is past. It is a miracle that anyone was spared. Ebenezer says we should not grieve for those who are gone; they were heroic; they saved the Fort. He says too, that we shall never again be troubled by Indians. Therefore let us forget and be happy. I have forgotten Miller. You can afford to do the same.”
“Yes, I forgive him.” Then, after a long silence, Alfred continued, “Will you go down to the old sycamore?”
Down the winding path they went. Coming to a steep place in the rocky bank Alfred jumped down and then turned to help Betty. But she avoided his gaze, pretended to not see his outstretched hands, and leaped lightly down beside him. He looked at her with perplexity and anxiety in his eyes. Before he could speak she ran on ahead of him and climbed down the bank to the pool. He followed slowly, thoughtfully. The supreme moment had come. He knew it, and somehow he did not feel the confidence the Colonel had inspired in him. It had been easy for him to think of subduing this imperious young lady; but when the time came to assert his will he found he could not remember what he had intended to say, and his feelings were divided between his love for her and the horrible fear that he should lose her.
When he reached the sycamore tree he found her sitting behind it with a cluster of yellow daisies in her lap. Alfred gazed at her, conscious that all his hopes of happiness were dependent on the next few words that would issue from her smiling lips.

===Chinese===

「照我想，那倒不算顶难，比不上爬进我的屋子差点要了我的命，也比不上从博格斯上尉房里偷火药。」
「后一桩，至少给了我一个出力的机会，」贝蒂说，带着她那股古灵精怪的调皮劲儿。
「那是女人干过的顶了不起的事，」阿尔弗雷德低声说。
「哦，哪里，我不过跑得快。」
「拿全世界换我都要看那一眼，可我那时躺在长凳上直想死了算了。我连朝枪眼望一眼的力气都没有。唉！那段可怕的日子！我一辈子忘不了。夜里睡不着，耳朵里是呐喊和枪声。跟着梦见在烧着的屋顶上跑，一切都活生生回来，火焰几乎燎得着脸，烧焦的木头味儿都闻得见。醒过来，就想起你被抱进棱堡、脸白得像死人的那一瞬——我当时只当你死了。」
「可我没死。我看那段可怕的日子，我们最好忘掉。过去了。谁都活下来，是奇迹。埃比尼泽说，不该为去了的人伤心；他们是英雄；是他们救了堡子。他还说，印第安人再也不会来搅扰我们了。所以让我们忘掉，快活起来。我把米勒忘了。你也忘得起。」
「是，我饶恕他。」沉默了好一阵，阿尔弗雷德又说：「到老梧桐树那儿去好吗？」
两人下了蜿蜒的小径。走到石岸一处陡地方，阿尔弗雷德先跳下去，回身要扶贝蒂。可她避开他的目光，只当没看见他伸出的手，轻轻巧巧跳在他身边。他望着她，眼里又急又惑。他还没开口，她已跑到前头，下岸到了水潭边。他慢慢跟着，心事重重。决断的时辰到了。他知道；可不知怎么，上校给他打的那点底气，这会儿全没了。想那骄傲的小姐如何如何，原也容易；真到亮出自己主意的时候，他发觉想好的话全忘了，心里一边是对她的爱，一边是怕失去她那可怕的恐慌，两下里拉扯。
走到梧桐树跟前，只见她坐在树后，膝上捧着一捧黄雏菊。阿尔弗雷德望着她，知道自己全部的幸福，全看她含笑的嘴唇接下来吐出的几个字。

===Original===

The little brown hands, which were now rather nervously arranging the flowers, held more than his life.
“Are they not sweet?” asked Betty, giving him a fleeting glance. “We call them ‘black-eyed Susans.’ Could anything be lovelier than that soft, dark brown?”
“Yes,” answered Alfred, looking into her eyes.
“But﻿—but you are not looking at my daisies at all,” said Betty, lowering her eyes.
“No, I am not,” said Alfred. Then suddenly: “A year ago this very day we were here.”
“Here? Oh, yes, I believe I do remember. It was the day we came in my canoe and had such fine fishing.”
“Is that all you remember?”
“I can recollect nothing in particular. It was so long ago.”
“I suppose you will say you had no idea why I wanted you to come to this spot in particular.”
“I supposed you simply wanted to take a walk, and it is very pleasant here.”
“Then Col. Zane did not tell you?” demanded Alfred. Receiving no reply he went on.
“Did you read my letter?”
“What letter?”
“The letter old Sam should have given you last fall. Did you read it?”
“Yes,” answered Betty, faintly.
“Did your brother tell you I wanted to see you this morning?”
“Yes, he told me, and it made me very angry,” said Betty, raising her head. There was a bright red spot in each cheek. “You﻿—you seemed to think you﻿—that I﻿—well﻿—I did not like it.”
“I think I understand; but you are entirely wrong. I have never thought you cared for me. My wildest dreams never left me any confidence. Col. Zane and Wetzel both had some deluded notion that you cared﻿—”
“But they had no right to say that or to think it,” said Betty, passionately. She sprang to her feet, scattering the daisies over the grass. “For them to presume that I cared for you is absurd. I never gave them any reason to think so, for﻿—for I﻿—I don’t.”
“Very well, then, there is nothing more to be said,” answered Alfred, in a voice that was calm and slightly cold. “I’m sorry if you have been annoyed. I have been mad, of course, but I promise you that you need fear no further annoyance from me. Come, I think we should return to the house.”
And he turned and walked slowly up the path. He had taken perhaps a dozen steps when she called him.
“Mr. Clarke, come back.”
Alfred retraced his steps and stood before her again. Then he saw a different Betty. The haughty poise had disappeared. Her head was bowed. Her little hands were tightly pressed over a throbbing bosom.

===Chinese===

那双棕色的小手正紧张地摆弄着花——它们攥着的，比他的命还重。
「香不香？」贝蒂瞟了他一眼，问。「我们管它们叫『黑眼苏珊』。还有什么比那柔和的深棕色更可爱？」
「有，」阿尔弗雷德答，眼睛望进她的眼睛。
「可——可你根本没看我的雏菊，」贝蒂垂下眼睛说。
「是，没看，」阿尔弗雷德说。忽然：「一年前的今天，我们在这儿。」
「这儿？哦，对了，我好像记得。是我们坐我的独木舟来、钓了那么多鱼的那天。」
「你就记得这些？」
「旁的记不得什么了。那么久的事了。」
「我猜你还要说，你不知道为什么偏要你来这个地方。」
「我当你不过是想散散步，这儿又正好玩。」
「这么说赞恩上校没告诉你？」阿尔弗雷德追问。没有回音，他说下去。
「我的信你读了没有？」
「什么信？」
「老萨姆去年秋天该交给你的那封信。读了没有？」
「读了，」贝蒂轻轻答。
「你哥哥今天早晨告诉你我要见你，说了没有？」
「说了。说得我好生气，」贝蒂抬起头，两腮各有一块亮亮的红。「你——你好像以为你——以为我——哼——我不爱听。」
「我想我明白；可你全想岔了。我从没当你心里有我。我最狂的梦也没给过我一点底气。赞恩上校和韦策尔倒都有个糊涂念头，说你心里——」
「可他们没有权利那么说、那么想，」贝蒂激愤地说。她跳起来，雏菊撒了一草地。「他们竟当我心里有你，荒唐。我从没给过他们一点由头这么想，因为——因为我——我心里没有。」
「很好，那就没什么可说的了，」阿尔弗雷德答道，声音平静而微冷。「搅扰了你，对不起。我自然是疯了；可我答应你，往后你再不用怕我搅扰。来，我们该回屋了。」
他转身顺小径慢慢往上走。走了十来步，她在后面喊他。
「克拉克先生，回来。」
阿尔弗雷德折回，又站到她面前。随即他看见了一个换了样的贝蒂。那骄傲的架势没了。她低着头，两只小手紧紧按在怦怦跳的胸口。

===Original===

“Well,” said Alfred, after a moment.
“Why﻿—why are you in such a hurry to go?”
“I have learned what I wanted to know. And after that I do not imagine I would be very agreeable. I am going back. Are you coming?”
“I did not mean quite﻿—quite what I said,” whispered Betty.
“Then what did you mean?” asked Alfred, in a stern voice.
“I don’t know. Please don’t speak so.”
“Betty, forgive my harshness. Can you expect a man to feel as I do and remain calm? You know I love you. You must not trifle any longer. You must not fight any longer.”
“But I can’t help fighting.”
“Look at me,” said Alfred, taking her hands. “Let me see your eyes. I believe you care a little for me, or else you wouldn’t have called me back. I love you. Can you understand that?”
“Yes, I can; and I think you should love me a great deal to make up for what you made me suffer.”
“Betty, look at me.”
Slowly she raised her head and lifted the downcast eyes. Those telltale traitors no longer hid her secret. With a glad cry Alfred caught her in his arms. She tried to hide her face, but he got his hand under her chin and held it firmly so that the sweet crimson lips were very near his own. Then he slowly bent his head.
Betty saw his intention, closed her eyes and whispered:
“Alfred, please don’t﻿—it’s not fair﻿—I beg of you﻿—Oh!”
That kiss was Betty’s undoing. She uttered a strange little cry. Then her dark head found a hiding place over his heart, and her slender form, which a moment before had resisted so fiercely, sank yielding into his embrace.
“Betty, do you dare tell me now that you do not care for me?” Alfred whispered into the dusky hair which rippled over his breast.
Betty was brave even in her surrender. Her hands moved slowly upward along his arms, slipped over his shoulders, and clasped round his neck. Then she lifted a flushed and tearstained face with tremulous lips and wonderful shining eyes.
“Alfred, I do love you﻿—with my whole heart I love you. I never knew until now.”
The hours flew apace. The prolonged ringing of the dinner bell brought the lovers back to earth, and to the realization that the world held others than themselves. Slowly they climbed the familiar path, but this time as never before. They walked hand in hand. From the bluff they looked back. They wanted to make sure they were not dreaming.

===Chinese===

「怎么，」过了一会儿，阿尔弗雷德说。
「干吗——干吗这样急着走？」
「我要知道的都知道了。知道了这个，我想我也讨人嫌了。我要回去。你来不来？」
「我方才的话，不完全是——完全是那个意思，」贝蒂悄声说。
「那是什么意思？」阿尔弗雷德声音很硬地问。
「我不知道。求你别这样说话。」
「贝蒂，原谅我粗声粗气。一个男人心里像我这样，你还指望他沉得住气？你知道我爱你。你不能再打马虎眼了。不能再打了。」
「可我忍不住不打。」
「看着我，」阿尔弗雷德握住她的手。「叫我看看你的眼睛。我信你心里到底有一点我，不然你不会喊我回来。我爱你。这个你懂不懂？」
「懂；我还想，你该加倍地爱我，补上你叫我受的这些罪。」
「贝蒂，看着我。」
她慢慢抬起头，垂着的眼睛也抬起来。这对泄密的叛徒，再也藏不住她的秘密了。阿尔弗雷德欢叫一声，把她搂进怀里。她想遮脸，他却把手托在她颏下，稳稳托住，那甜甜的红嘴唇离他自己的那样近。随后他慢慢低下头。
贝蒂看出了他的意思，闭上眼，悄声说：
「阿尔弗雷德，求你别——这不公道——我求你——哦！」
那一吻把贝蒂降服了。她发出一声奇异的轻呼。随后那颗乌黑的头，在他心口上寻着了藏身处；那先前还倔强抗拒的纤细身子，软软地偎进了他的怀抱。
「贝蒂，这会儿你还敢说你心里没有我？」阿尔弗雷德对着那披散在他胸前的黑发低声问。
贝蒂连降服也降服得勇敢。她的双手顺着他两臂慢慢往上移，滑过肩头，搂住他的脖颈。随后她仰起一张绯红带泪的脸，嘴唇颤着，眼睛亮得出奇。
「阿尔弗雷德，我真的爱你——拿我整个心爱你。到这会儿我才知道。」
时光飞逝。开饭铃响个不停，把一对恋人唤回人间，叫他们想起世上还有别人。他们慢慢爬上熟路，这一回却是从来没有过的走法。他们手拉着手。到了崖顶，他们回头望，要看看真切，这不是梦。

===Original===

The water rushed over the fall more musically than ever before; the white patches of foam floated round and round the shady pool; the leaves of the sycamore rustled cheerily in the breeze. On a dead branch a woodpecker hammered industriously.
“Before we get out of sight of that dear old tree I want to make a confession,” said Betty, as she stood before Alfred. She was pulling at the fringe on his hunting coat.
“You need not make confessions to me.”
“But this was dreadful; it preys on my conscience.”
“Very well, I will be your judge. Your punishment shall be slight.”
“One day when you were lying unconscious from your wound, Bessie sent me to watch you. I nursed you for hours, and﻿—and﻿—do not think badly of me﻿—I﻿—I kissed you.”
“My darling,” cried the enraptured young man.
When they at last reached the house they found Col. Zane on the doorstep.
“Where on earth have you been?” he said. “Wetzel was here. He said he would not wait to see you. There he goes up the hill. He is behind that laurel.”
They looked and presently saw the tall figure of the hunter emerge from the bushes. He stopped and leaned on his rifle. For a minute he remained motionless. Then he waved his hand and plunged into the thicket. Betty sighed and Alfred said:
“Poor Wetzel! ever restless, ever roaming.”
“Hello, there!” exclaimed a gay voice. The lovers turned to see the smiling face of Isaac, and over his shoulder Myeerah’s happy face beaming on them. “Alfred, you are a lucky dog. You can thank Myeerah and me for this; because if I had not taken to the river and nearly drowned myself to give you that opportunity you would not wear that happy face today. Blush away, Betts, it becomes you mightily.”
“Bessie, here they are!” cried Col. Zane, in his hearty voice. “She is tamed at last. No excuses, Alfred, in to dinner you go.”
Col. Zane pushed the young people up the steps before him, and stopping on the threshold while he knocked the ashes from his pipe, he smiled contentedly.

===Chinese===

水从瀑上跌下来，比往日更好听；白沫一团团，绕着幽暗的水潭打转；梧桐的叶子在风里欢快地响。一根枯枝上，啄木鸟笃笃地忙碌。
「趁那棵亲爱的老树还没出眼，我要招认一件事，」贝蒂站在阿尔弗雷德跟前说，一面揪着他猎衣上的穗子。
「你用不着向我招认什么。」
「可这件了不得；它咬着我的良心。」
「好，我做你的法官。判得轻些就是。」
「有一天，你受了伤，昏昏沉沉躺着，贝茜打发我去守着你。我看护了你几个钟头，后来——后来——你别把我想坏——我——我吻了你。」
「我的宝贝！」小伙子心花怒放地喊。
他们终于回到家门口，见赞恩上校坐在台阶上。
「你们到底上哪儿去了？」他说。「韦策尔来过。他说不等你们了。瞧，他上坡了。在那丛月桂后头。」
他们望去，一会儿看见猎人高大的身影从灌木里出来。他停住，拄着枪立了一分钟，一动不动。随后他挥了挥手，一头扎进树丛里去了。贝蒂叹了口气，阿尔弗雷德说：
「可怜的韦策尔！永远不安生，永远在路上。」
「喂，你们哪！」一个欢快的声音喊。恋人回头，见艾萨克笑吟吟的脸，他肩后米伊拉正喜盈盈地望着他们。「阿尔弗雷德，你这条走运的狗。这得谢米伊拉和我；要不是我跳下河、差点淹死，给你挣下那个机会，你今天也戴不上这副快活脸。红吧，贝茨，红脸再衬你没有了。」
「贝茜，他们来了！」赞恩上校声音洪亮地喊。「她到底服帖了。别推辞，阿尔弗雷德，进屋吃饭去。」
赞恩上校把年轻人推上台阶，自己站在门槛上磕了磕烟斗里的灰，心满意足地笑了。
